```

Read file /home/agent/Code/KSE-Agentic-AI-stage2/src/models.py, lines 1-162
1: """Model ladder for the alert forecasts: two baselines, then two learners.
2: 
3: Both learners read the same `FEATURES` matrix built by `src/features.build` (the daily
4: learners read its day-level subset, `DAY_FEATURES`), so the report can compare *learners*
5: rather than two feature pipelines. Everything here is a pure function or an sklearn
6: factory: no classes, no state outside the fitted sklearn estimator, nothing fitted on
7: anything but the training window.
8: """
9: 
10: from __future__ import annotations
11: 
12: import numpy as np
13: import pandas as pd
14: from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingRegressor
15: from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression, PoissonRegressor
16: 
17: from src.features import FEATURES
18: 
19: # Daily targets have one row per origin x region, so the hourly features carry no
20: # day-level information: `hour_local` is constant-noise across the collapse,
21: # `minutes_to_sunset`/`is_night` describe one specific hour of the 24. They must not enter
22: # a daily model (they would fit the arbitrary "first hour" of the day). `day_of_week`,
23: # `is_weekend`, `month`, `is_holiday`, `day_length_min` are properties of the origin's
24: # local calendar day, not of an hour, so they stay.
25: HOUR_FEATURES = ["hour_local", "minutes_to_sunset", "is_night"]
26: DAY_FEATURES = [f for f in FEATURES if f not in HOUR_FEATURES]
27: 
28: _PROFILE_KEYS = ["region", "day_of_week", "hour_local"]
29: 
30: 
31: def daily_panel(frame: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
32:     """Collapse a `build()` frame to one row per origin x region.
33: 
34:     Day features are taken verbatim from the origin's first (00:00 local) hour row, not
35:     aggregated, so the panel describes the calendar day the forecast is made on. Labels are
36:     summed over the 24 forecast hours. No row is fabricated: the collapsed index is exactly
37:     the origins x regions present in `frame`.
38:     """
39:     first = frame.sort_values(["origin", "region", "hour"]).groupby(["origin", "region"], as_index=False).head(1)
40:     sums = frame.groupby(["origin", "region"], as_index=False).agg(
41:         y_starts_day=("y_start", "sum"), y_minutes_day=("y_minutes", "sum")
42:     )
43:     out = first[["origin", "region", *DAY_FEATURES]].merge(sums, on=["origin", "region"], how="inner")
44:     return out.sort_values(["origin", "region"]).reset_index(drop=True)
45: 
46: 
47: def hour_of_week_profile(train_frame: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
48:     """Baseline A: empirical P(alert starts) per (region, local weekday, local hour).
49: 
50:     Fit on training rows only. The two fallback levels are precomputed here as broadcast
51:     columns, so prediction is one lookup and one map — no recompute that could see eval data.
52:     """
53:     cells = train_frame.groupby(_PROFILE_KEYS, as_index=False)["y_start"].mean().rename(columns={"y_start": "p"})
54:     cells["p_region"] = cells["region"].map(train_frame.groupby("region")["y_start"].mean())
55:     cells["p_global"] = float(train_frame["y_start"].mean())
56:     return cells
57: 
58: 
59: def predict_hour_of_week(profile: pd.DataFrame, frame: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:
60:     """Baseline A prediction: cell mean -> region mean -> global mean. Length == len(frame).
61: 
62:     The two fallback levels live as broadcast columns of `profile`, so they are read with a
63:     separate map instead of the cell merge (a left join would NaN them out on an unseen cell).
64:     """
65:     cells = profile[_PROFILE_KEYS + ["p"]]
66:     region_mean = profile.drop_duplicates("region").set_index("region")["p_region"]
67:     global_mean = float(profile["p_global"].iloc[0]) if len(profile) else 0.0
68:     merged = frame[_PROFILE_KEYS].merge(cells, on=_PROFILE_KEYS, how="left")
69:     filled = merged["p"].fillna(merged["region"].map(region_mean)).fillna(global_mean)
70:     return filled.to_numpy(np.float64)
71: 
72: 
73: def seasonal_naive(train_frame: pd.DataFrame, frame: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:
74:     """Baseline B: the `y_start` observed at hour - 7 days for the same region; 0.0 if the
75:     training window does not cover that timestamp (quiet regime / start of the series)."""
76:     lookup = train_frame.groupby(["region", "hour"])["y_start"].mean()
77:     key = pd.MultiIndex.from_frame(frame[["region", "hour"]].assign(hour=frame["hour"] - pd.Timedelta("7D")))
78:     return lookup.reindex(key).fillna(0.0).to_numpy(np.float64)
79: 
80: 
81: def duration_rows(frame: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
82:     """Rows where the duration label is defined: a start happened and was neither censored
83:     (`naive` end) nor implausibly long (>24 h), so excluding them avoids training on imputed
84:     ends."""
85:     return frame[(frame["y_start"] == 1) & frame["y_dur_log"].notna()]
86: 
87: 
88: def duration_naive(train_frame: pd.DataFrame, frame: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:
89:     """Duration baseline: training-window median log-duration-at-start, broadcast.
90: 
91:     ponytail: one constant per region-pooled series, no timing signal; upgrade path: make the
92:     median conditional on hour/region once the empirical quantiles show real spread.
93:     """
94:     median = duration_rows(train_frame)["y_dur_log"].median()
95:     return np.full(len(frame), 0.0 if pd.isna(median) else float(median))
96: 
97: 
98: def make_poisson(**kw) -> PoissonRegressor:
99:     """Model 1 (counts/minutes): log-link Poisson, coefficients expoundable as log-rate
100:     effects. Target must be >= 0 (y_starts_day, y_minutes_day). `alpha` is the L2 penalty
101:     that keeps the unscaled lag magnitudes in check. `newton-cholesky` because the raw
102:     (unscaled) lag features make lbfgs crawl (thousands of iterations); the exact Hessian
103:     converges in ~10 and is deterministic, so no seed is needed.
104:     ponytail: newton-cholesky factorizes a p x p matrix, so it wants features < rows;
105:     upgrade path: switch to lbfgs + a train-fitted scaler if the feature count ever grows.
106:     """
107:     params = dict(alpha=1.0, solver="newton-cholesky", max_iter=1000)
108:     params.update(kw)
109:     return PoissonRegressor(**params)
110: 
111: 
112: def make_logistic(**kw) -> LogisticRegression:
113:     """Model 1 (hourly start probability). Features are used raw: no scaler, so coefficients
114:     stay log-odds per original unit (a scaler fitted on train would be fine leakage-wise but
115:     would cost the interpretability that is the point of Model 1). `newton-cholesky` for the
116:     same reason as the Poisson fit. Seed fixed for the rare stochastic solvers.
117:     """
118:     params = dict(C=1.0, solver="newton-cholesky", max_iter=1000, random_state=0)
119:     params.update(kw)
120:     return LogisticRegression(**params)
121: 
122: 
123: def make_histgb(loss: str = "poisson", quantile: float | None = None, **kw) -> HistGradientBoostingRegressor:
124:     """Model 2: gradient boosting on the same matrix. `loss="poisson"` for counts/minutes,
125:     `loss="quantile"` with q in {0.1, 0.5, 0.9} for log-duration. Handles NaN natively, so
126:     the imputation in `fit_predict` is only needed by the linear models. `early_stopping` is
127:     off: an internal random validation split would make the fit depend on row order.
128:     """
129:     params = dict(
130:         loss=loss,
131:         learning_rate=0.05,
132:         max_iter=300,
133:         max_leaf_nodes=31,
134:         min_samples_leaf=20,
135:         l2_regularization=1.0,
136:         early_stopping=False,
137:         random_state=0,
138:     )
139:     if loss == "quantile":
140:         if quantile not in (0.1, 0.5, 0.9):
141:             raise ValueError(f"quantile must be 0.1/0.5/0.9, got {quantile!r}")
142:         params["quantile"] = quantile  # target range: log1p(minutes), ~[0, log(1440)]
143:     params.update(kw)
144:     return HistGradientBoostingRegressor(**params)
145: 
146: 
147: def fit_predict(model, train_frame: pd.DataFrame, frame: pd.DataFrame, target: str) -> np.ndarray:
148:     """Fit `model` on train rows, predict `frame` on the same feature matrix.
149: 
150:     Hourly frames carry all of `FEATURES`; daily panels carry `DAY_FEATURES`. Selecting the
151:     columns present keeps one code path for both. NaN is possible in `hours_since_last_alert`
152:     for the first days of the series; the linear models reject it, so it is filled with the
153:     train median (an imputer fit on the training window only — never on the eval frame).
154:     """
155:     cols = [c for c in FEATURES if c in train_frame.columns]
156:     assert len(cols) in (len(FEATURES), len(DAY_FEATURES)), "unexpected feature columns"
157:     X = train_frame[cols].to_numpy(np.float64)
158:     median = np.nanmedian(X, axis=0)
159:     median = np.where(np.isnan(median), 0.0, median)  # all-NaN column in train: 0 is as good as any
160:     model.fit(np.where(np.isnan(X), median, X), train_frame[target].to_numpy())
161:     X_eval = frame[cols].to_numpy(np.float64)
162:     return model.predict(np.where(np.isnan(X_eval), median, X_eval))

1: """Exploratory analysis of Ukrainian air-raid alerts: seasonality, clustering, gaps,
2: regime shift and per-oblast burden. Writes figures to `reports/figures/` and the numbers
3: to `reports/eda.md`.
4: 
5: Every helper is pure and takes the frames from `src.features`, so the whole module is
6: reproducible from `python -m src.eda` and testable offline. Windows are Europe/Kyiv local
7: (the forecast origin convention) unless a column is explicitly UTC.
8: 
9: ponytail: single hard-coded 28-day window for the change point; upgrade path: sweep the
10: window and report a stability band.
11: """
12: 
13: from __future__ import annotations
14: 
15: import pathlib
16: 
17: import matplotlib
18: 
19: matplotlib.use("Agg")  # non-interactive: must be set before pyplot import
20: import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
21: import numpy as np
22: import pandas as pd
23: 
24: from src.features import KYIV, hourly_grid, load_alerts
25: 
26: FIGDIR = pathlib.Path("reports/figures")
27: REPORT = pathlib.Path("reports/eda.md")
28: WEEKDAYS = ["Mon", "Tue", "Wed", "Thu", "Fri", "Sat", "Sun"]
29: 
30: 
31: def daily_starts(grid: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
32:     """National daily alert-starts and alert-minutes from the dense region×hour grid.
33: 
34:     Days are Europe/Kyiv calendar days, matching the forecast origin convention.
35:     """
36:     g = grid.copy()
37:     g["date"] = g["hour"].dt.tz_convert(KYIV).dt.normalize().dt.tz_localize(None)
38:     out = g.groupby("date").agg(starts=("starts", "sum"), minutes=("minutes", "sum"))
39:     return out.sort_index().reset_index()
40: 
41: 
42: def hour_of_week_share(alerts: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
43:     """7×24 share of national alert-starts by local day-of-week (Mon=0) × local hour."""
44:     local = alerts["started_at"].dt.tz_convert(KYIV)
45:     counts = pd.crosstab(
46:         local.dt.dayofweek.astype(int), local.dt.hour.astype(int)
47:     ).reindex(index=range(7), columns=range(24), fill_value=0)
48:     return counts / counts.to_numpy().sum()
49: 
50: 
51: def gap_hours(alerts: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.Series:
52:     """Hours between consecutive alert-starts within each region (quiet gaps)."""
53:     a = alerts.sort_values(["region", "started_at"])
54:     return (a.groupby("region")["started_at"].diff().dt.total_seconds() / 3600.0).dropna()
55: 
56: 
57: def burden(alerts: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
58:     """Per-region burden: alert count, alert-minutes, alerts per 100 days.
59: 
60:     Alert-minutes sum `duration_min`, which is NaN for right-censored and >24h alerts, so
61:     those contribute 0 minutes but still count as a start (see `censored_share`).
62:     """
63:     a = alerts.sort_values(["region", "started_at"])
64:     span_days = a.groupby("region")["started_at"].agg(
65:         lambda s: (s.max() - s.min()).total_seconds() / 86400.0
66:     )
67:     out = pd.DataFrame(
68:         {
69:             "starts": a.groupby("region").size(),
70:             "alert_minutes": a.groupby("region")["duration_min"].sum(),
71:             "censored_share": a.groupby("region")["duration_min"].apply(
72:                 lambda s: float(s.isna().mean())
73:             ),
74:         }
75:     )
76:     out["starts_per_100d"] = out["starts"] / span_days * 100.0
77:     return out.sort_values("alert_minutes", ascending=False)
78: 
79: 
80: def regime_break(daily: pd.DataFrame, window: int = 28) -> dict:
81:     """Single change point of national daily starts: split maximising next-vs-previous mean.
82: 
83:     Compares the mean of the `window` days after a candidate split with the `window` days
84:     before it and returns the split with the largest increase.
85:     """
86:     x = daily.set_index("date")["starts"].asfreq("D").fillna(0.0)
87:     before = x.rolling(window).mean()
88:     after = x.rolling(window).mean().shift(-window)
89:     diff = after - before
90:     date = diff.idxmax()
91:     return {"date": date, "before": float(before.loc[date]), "after": float(after.loc[date])}
92: 
93: 
94: def censored_share(alerts: pd.DataFrame) -> dict:
95:     """Share of starts that are `naive` (end never arrived) vs. duration-censored (incl. >24h)."""
96:     return {
97:         "naive": float(alerts["naive"].mean()),
98:         "duration": float(alerts["duration_min"].isna().mean()),
99:     }
100: 
101: 
102: def year_rate(daily: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
103:     """Starts-per-day by calendar year (fair across partial years like the current one)."""
104:     y = daily.assign(year=daily["date"].dt.year).groupby("year")
105:     return (y["starts"].sum() / y.size()).rename("starts_per_day").reset_index()
106: 
107: 
108: def _plot_daily(daily: pd.DataFrame, path: pathlib.Path) -> None:
109:     fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(12, 4))
110:     ax.plot(daily["date"], daily["starts"], lw=0.5, alpha=0.4, label="daily starts")
111:     ax.plot(
112:         daily["date"],
113:         daily["starts"].rolling(28, min_periods=1).mean(),
114:         lw=1.8,
115:         color="crimson",
116:         label="28-day mean",
117:     )
118:     ax.set(title="National air-raid alert starts per day", ylabel="starts / day")
119:     ax.legend()
120:     fig.tight_layout()
121:     fig.savefig(path, dpi=110)
122:     plt.close(fig)
123: 
124: 
125: def _plot_hour_of_week(share: pd.DataFrame, peak: tuple[int, int], path: pathlib.Path) -> None:
126:     fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(11, 3.6))
127:     im = ax.imshow(share.to_numpy() * 100.0, aspect="auto", cmap="magma")
128:     ax.set(
129:         xticks=range(24),
130:         xticklabels=[f"{h:02d}" for h in range(24)],
131:         yticks=range(7),
132:         yticklabels=WEEKDAYS,
133:         xlabel="hour (Europe/Kyiv)",
134:         title="Share of alert starts by local day-of-week × hour (%)",
135:     )
136:     ax.set_xticks(np.arange(-0.5, 24, 1), minor=True)
137:     ax.set_yticks(np.arange(-0.5, 7, 1), minor=True)
138:     ax.grid(which="minor", color="w", lw=0.3)
139:     ax.tick_params(which="minor", length=0)
140:     ax.scatter([peak[1]], [peak[0]], s=80, facecolors="none", edgecolors="cyan")
141:     fig.colorbar(im, ax=ax, label="% of all starts")
142:     fig.tight_layout()
143:     fig.savefig(path, dpi=110)
144:     plt.close(fig)
145: 
146: 
147: def _plot_monthly(daily: pd.DataFrame, path: pathlib.Path) -> None:
148:     m = daily.assign(month=daily["date"].dt.to_period("M").dt.to_timestamp())
149:     agg = m.groupby("month").agg(starts=("starts", "sum"), minutes=("minutes", "mean"))
150:     fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(12, 4))
151:     ax.bar(agg.index, agg["starts"], width=20, color="steelblue", label="starts / month")
152:     ax.set(ylabel="alert starts / month", title="Monthly alert starts and mean alert-minutes per day")
153:     ax2 = ax.twinx()
154:     ax2.plot(agg.index, agg["minutes"], color="darkorange", lw=1.8, label="mean min / day")
155:     ax2.set_ylabel("mean alert-minutes / day", color="darkorange")
156:     ax.legend(loc="upper left")
157:     ax2.legend(loc="upper right")
158:     fig.tight_layout()
159:     fig.savefig(path, dpi=110)
160:     plt.close(fig)
161: 
162: 
163: def _plot_gaps(gaps: pd.Series, since_days: pd.Series, path: pathlib.Path) -> None:
164:     fig, (ax1, ax2) = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(12, 4))
165:     bins = np.logspace(np.log10(max(gaps.min(), 1e-3)), np.log10(gaps.max()), 60)
166:     ax1.hist(gaps, bins=bins, color="teal")
167:     ax1.set_xscale("log")
168:     ax1.set(xlabel="gap between consecutive alerts (h)", ylabel="count", title="Quiet gaps (log scale)")
169:     ax2.barh(since_days.sort_values().index, since_days.sort_values().to_numpy(), color="slateblue")
170:     ax2.set(xlabel="days since last alert (at data end)", title="Current quiet stretch per region")
171:     ax2.tick_params(axis="y", labelsize=7)
172:     fig.tight_layout()
173:     fig.savefig(path, dpi=110)
174:     plt.close(fig)
175: 
176: 
177: def _plot_burden(b: pd.DataFrame, path: pathlib.Path) -> None:
178:     s = b.sort_values("alert_minutes")
179:     fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 6))
180:     ax.barh(s.index, s["alert_minutes"], color="indianred")
181:     ax.set(xlabel="total known alert-minutes (2022–2026)", title="Per-oblast alert burden")
182:     fig.tight_layout()
183:     fig.savefig(path, dpi=110)
184:     plt.close(fig)
185: 
186: 
187: def _plot_durations(alerts: pd.DataFrame, cens: dict, path: pathlib.Path) -> None:
188:     d = alerts["duration_min"].dropna()
189:     fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4))
190:     bins = np.logspace(np.log10(0.5), np.log10(d.max() + 1), 50)
191:     ax.hist(d, bins=bins, color="seagreen")
192:     ax.set_xscale("log")
193:     ax.axvline(30, color="crimson", lw=1.2, ls="--")
194:     ax.text(31, ax.get_ylim()[1] * 0.8, "30 min (censored imputation)", color="crimson", fontsize=8)
195:     ax.set(
196:         xlabel="alert duration (min, log scale)",
197:         ylabel="count",
198:         title=f"Alert durations — {cens['duration']:.1%} censored (excluded from this histogram)",
199:     )
200:     fig.tight_layout()
201:     fig.savefig(path, dpi=110)
202:     plt.close(fig)
203: 
204: 
205: def analyse(alerts: pd.DataFrame, grid: pd.DataFrame) -> dict:
206:     """Compute every number reported in `reports/eda.md`."""
207:     daily = daily_starts(grid)
208:     share = hour_of_week_share(alerts)
209:     peak_dow, peak_hour = np.unravel_index(share.to_numpy().argmax(), share.shape)
210:     gap = gap_hours(alerts)
211:     b = burden(alerts)
212:     cens = censored_share(alerts)
213:     rate = year_rate(daily)
214:     brk = regime_break(daily)
215: 
216:     last = (
217:         alerts.groupby("region")["started_at"]
218:         .max()
219:         .dt.tz_convert(KYIV)
220:         .dt.tz_localize(None)
221:         .dt.normalize()
222:     )
223:     since = (daily["date"].max() - last).dt.total_seconds() / 86400.0
224:     return {
225:         "daily": daily,
226:         "share": share,
227:         "peak": (int(peak_dow), int(peak_hour)),
228:         "gaps": gap,
229:         "burden": b,
230:         "censored": cens,
231:         "rate": rate,
232:         "break": brk,
233:         "since_days": since,
234:     }
235: 
236: 
237: def main() -> None:
238:     FIGDIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
239:     alerts = load_alerts()
240:     grid = hourly_grid(alerts)
241:     r = analyse(alerts, grid)
242: 
243:     _plot_daily(r["daily"], FIGDIR / "daily_starts.png")
244:     _plot_hour_of_week(r["share"], r["peak"], FIGDIR / "hour_of_week_heatmap.png")
245:     _plot_monthly(r["daily"], FIGDIR / "monthly_burden.png")
246:     _plot_gaps(r["gaps"], r["since_days"], FIGDIR / "gaps_and_quiet.png")
247:     _plot_burden(r["burden"], FIGDIR / "region_burden.png")
248:     _plot_durations(alerts, r["censored"], FIGDIR / "durations.png")
249: 
250:     _report(alerts, r)
251: 
252:

_Thinking:_

Now I see the Model 1 logistic is clearly broken: per-region predictions are exactly 0.0 or 1.0 (except Kyivska 0.196, Mykolaivska 0.696). That's a saturated/degenerate fit — the logistic with newton-cholesky on raw unscaled features with C=0.1 is producing extreme probabilities, and Brier (0.213) is worse than the base rate (0.172). This is a bug in the Model 1 fit, not an honest finding.

Why? `newton-cholesky` with `C=0.1`... The features include `hour_local` (0-23), `hours_since_last_alert` (0-2160), `alert_minutes_past_24h` etc. Unscaled, and with strong L2 (C=0.1 → alpha=10) — that shouldn't saturate. Hmm, but per-region predictions exactly 0/1 with n=336 rows each: the model is essentially predicting by region and the *within-region* variance is 0.

Wait — actually the calibration "bins" show only 2 bins: pred 0.0 (n=7092) and pred 1.0 (n=972). So most predictions are exactly 0.0 or 1.0. A logistic regression with L2 regularization can't produce exactly 0.0 or 1.0 unless coefficients are huge (like ±50). With C=0.1 that's odd.

Hmm — unless the features are huge. `hours_since_last_alert` up to 2160. A coefficient of 0.01 gives 21.6 logits. So saturation is plausible: newton-cholesky with L2 C=0.1 (alpha=10) on features spanning 0-2160... the penalty is on the raw scale so small coefficients suffice → yes, extreme logits are possible.

So the interpretable linear model needs feature scaling to be sensible. The models.py author explicitly chose NOT to scale to preserve interpretability, but that yields a degenerate fit. Actually no — the issue is more that with unscaled features the L2 penalty is meaningless and newton-cholesky converges to a saturated solution.

The fix: scale features for the linear models using a scaler fit on the training window (leak-free, and coefficients remain interpretable per scaled unit — or we can report standardized coefficients). AGENTS/GOAL: "Fit preprocessing (scalers, encoders, imputers) on the training window only." So scaling is allowed if fit on train.

The models.py docstring argues against scaling for interpretability, but the result is a model that's worse than a constant predictor — that's not acceptable for "Model 1 (interpretable)". 

I should fix this. Options:
(a) Add a `StandardScaler`-like transform inside `fit_predict` for the linear models only, fit on train. But `fit_predict` is shared with HistGB (which doesn't need it) — add a parameter `scale=False`.
(b) Use `sklearn.pipeline.make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression(...))` in `make_logistic`/`make_poisson`. But then `fit_predict` does `train_frame[cols]` → pipeline handles it. That's clean and leak-safe (scaler fit inside pipeline on train only).

Hmm — but models.py is already written and committed. I need to modify it. The user said verify results myself; fixing a genuine bug is part of that. Also the coefficients would be in standardized units → still expoundable (per 1 SD).

But wait: is scaling really the cause? Let me verify empirically before changing. Let me run a quick experiment: fit logistic on the holdout train window with and without scaling, compare Brier.

Also Poisson: alpha=10 chosen, MAE 2.03 vs HistGB 1.53 — Poisson is fine (not degenerate).

Now let me test the logistic degeneracy hypothesis. Quick experiment: build a 60-day train and 14-day test, fit logistic raw vs standardized, compare Brier.

Let me write the script.

Note that the model's predictions being exactly 0.0/1.0 for whole regions: that suggests the logistic is separating regions perfectly, i.e., the model found that `trailing_28d_starts_per_day` (or similar) perfectly predicts. With C=0.1 (strong regularization) that shouldn't happen... unless newton-cholesky isn't applying the penalty correctly, or the features have massive scale making the penalty negligible relative to the loss.

Actually, `C=0.1` in sklearn means alpha = 1/C = 10 penalty on ||w||². With unscaled features, gradient magnitudes are huge for big features, so the optimizer's relative penalty is small → effectively unregularized → separable → extreme probabilities. Yes. Standardization fixes this.

Let me verify quickly.

Let me look at it from a different angle: maybe the extreme predictions come from `y_start` being fit but the *model* is fine, and the saturation is in the *majority* direction: p≈0 for 97% of rows. p=0.0 exactly means logit ≤ -745. With 20 features and coefficients summing... let me just compute.

Actually, WAIT. I bet the issue is `np.where(np.isnan(X), median, X)`: `median` is a 1-D array of length n_features, and `np.where(cond, median, X)` broadcasts fine. OK.

Actually, let me reconsider the whole approach. Time is being spent. The pragmatic fix: Model 1 (interpretable) should be a *sane* linear model. The problem may be that the features are highly collinear and the solver with newton-cholesky on raw data... but scaled also saturated identically, which is a strong hint that the *data* is nearly separable in a specific direction: `month` (train 8-9, test 9-10) — month coefficient 0.286 raw / 1.843 scaled for hours_since.

Hmm, but saturation identical for raw and scaled → the same extreme rows. Suspicious: maybe those rows have NaN in `hours_since_last_alert` imputed with the train median... no.

Let me just look at the actual extreme rows. One quick probe.

Actually, maybe simpler explanation: `fit_predict` returns `model.predict(...)` — for `LogisticRegression`, `predict` returns the *class label* (0/1), NOT the probability!!! 

YES! That's it. `LogisticRegression.predict()` returns class labels. `predict_proba` returns probabilities. `fit_predict` calls `model.predict(...)`, so for the logistic model it returns 0/1 labels — explaining exactly 0.0/1.0 predictions, the terrible Brier (which is then just the 0-1 loss = 0.213 = 1 - accuracy... indeed 1-0.7945 = 0.2055 ≈ Brier 0.213 on the holdout).

That's the bug: `fit_predict` uses `.predict()` where a probability is needed. For PoissonRegressor and HistGB, `predict()` returns the rate, which is what we want. For LogisticRegression, we need `predict_proba`[:, 1].

So Model 1's hourly start probability is actually a hard 0/1 label → hence "saturated". The fix: in `fit_predict`, if the estimator has `predict_proba` and the target is binary, use `predict_proba(...)[:, 1]`. Or add a `proba=False` parameter.

Cleanest minimal fix: in `models.py::fit_predict`, add parameter `proba: bool = False`; when True, call `model.predict_proba(X)[:, 1]`. Then `evaluate.py` calls `fit_predict(make_logistic(...), train, eval, "y_start", proba=True)`.

But careful: during hyperparameter selection, `fit_predict(make_logistic(C=c), tr, ev, "y_start")` also needs proba=True, otherwise F1 is computed on hard labels (which for thresholds is meaningless).

Let me implement: make `fit_predict` detect: if the fitted estimator has `predict_proba` (i.e., is a classifier) → return positive-class probability. That's automatic and correct: a classifier's "prediction" for our purposes is always a probability. HistGBRegressor has no `predict_proba`. So:

```python
    if hasattr(model, "predict_proba"):
        return model.predict_proba(X_eval)[:, 1]
    return model.predict(X_eval)
```

That's a 2-line fix, automatic, and can't be forgotten by callers. 

But note: for the *daily* Poisson/HistGB, `predict` is right. Good.

Also the `hour_of_week` baseline already returns a rate (probability), so thresholds are comparable.

Let me also double check that `duration` and `counts` paths use regressors — yes.

This also affects `choose_hyperparameters`' logistic C selection (currently a 3-way tie at 0.392 F1 — because hard labels at threshold 0.05 select all predicted-1 rows, and C barely changes the hard labels). With probabilities, the C choice becomes meaningful.

Great — genuine bug found by my verification. Let me fix models.py, re-run evaluate, and re-verify.

Let me also check the other places where the logistic probability is used: `brier`, `calibration`, `best_threshold` — all will now get proper probabilities.

Additionally, I noticed `hourly_mae` for histgb (0.302) is computed as MAE between prob and 0/1 label — fine.

Let me make the fix and also add a regression test in tests/test_models.py asserting `fit_predict` on a LogisticRegression returns probabilities in [0,1] with more than two distinct values.

73: def usable_origins(alerts: pd.DataFrame) -> list[pd.Timestamp]:
74:     """Daily origins whose whole 24 h horizon is observed: `origin + 24h <= max(finished_at)`.
75: 
76:     The last alerts end ~2026-10-11 04:46 UTC, so the last usable origin is Kyiv 2026-10-10.
77:     """
78:     last = alerts["finished_at"].max()
79:     return [o for o in origins(alerts["started_at"].min(), last) if o + HOUR <= last]
80: 
81: 
82: def _between(all_origins: list[pd.Timestamp], start, end) -> list[pd.Timestamp]:
83:     return [o for o in all_origins if start <= o < end]
84: 
85: 
86: def train_window(all_origins: list[pd.Timestamp], end, days: int = TRAIN_DAYS) -> list[pd.Timestamp]:
87:     """The last `days` daily origins strictly before `end` (rolling window, same for all)."""
88:     return _between(all_origins, end - pd.Timedelta(days=days), end)
89: 
90: 
91: def split_days(alerts: pd.DataFrame, test_days: int = TEST_DAYS, tune_days: int = TUNE_DAYS) -> dict:
92:     """Time-ordered, disjoint, non-overlapping windows: train < tune < test.
93: 
94:     `test` is the last `test_days` usable origins, `tune` the `tune_days` before it, `train`
95:     everything earlier. Nothing is shuffled.
96:     """
97:     usable = usable_origins(alerts)
98:     test = usable[-test_days:]
99:     tune = usable[-test_days - tune_days : -test_days]
100:     train = usable[: -test_days - tune_days]
101:     return {
102:         "train": train,
103:         "tune": tune,
104:         "test": test,
105:         "train_end": train[-1],
106:         "tune_start": tune[0],
107:         "test_start": test[0],
108:     }
109: 
110: 
111: def rolling_folds(
112:     alerts: pd.DataFrame, n_folds: int = N_FOLDS, fold_days: int = FOLD_DAYS, train_days: int = TRAIN_DAYS
113: ) -> list[dict]:
114:     """Rolling-origin backtest for hyperparameter choice only.
115: 
116:     Fold 0 predicts the 14 days immediately before the tune window; each later fold steps
117:     back another 14 days. Every fold trains on the `train_days` origins before its eval
118:     window. Because the newest fold ends at `tune_start`, neither `tune` nor `test` can
119:     appear in any fold.
120:     """
121:     usable = usable_origins(alerts)
122:     boundary = split_days(alerts)["tune_start"]
123:     folds = []
124:     for k in range(n_folds):
125:         end = boundary - pd.Timedelta(days=fold_days * k)
126:         start = end - pd.Timedelta(days=fold_days)
127:         folds.append(
128:             {"fold": k, "train": train_window(usable, start, train_days), "eval": _between(usable, start, end)}
129:         )
130:     return folds
131:

134: 
135: 
136: def _build_one(origin: pd.Timestamp) -> pd.DataFrame:
137:     return build(origin)
138: 
139: 
140: def build_frames(days: list[pd.Timestamp], workers: int = 4) -> pd.DataFrame:
141:     """Concatenated `build()` frames for `days`, sorted by origin (deterministic).
142: 
143:     One `build` call is ~1 s, so the ~250 origins a 3-fold backtest needs are built in
144:     parallel over forks; the parent warms `load_alerts/load_grid/load_weather` first so the
145:     forked workers inherit the caches instead of reloading the grid.
146:     """
147:     days = sorted(days)
148:     if not days:
149:         return pd.DataFrame()
150:     if workers > 1 and len(days) > 1:
151:         ctx = mp.get_context("fork")
152:         with ProcessPoolExecutor(max_workers=workers, mp_context=ctx) as ex:
153:             frames = list(ex.map(_build_one, days, chunksize=2))
154:     else:
155:         frames = [_build_one(o) for o in days]
156:     return pd.concat(frames, ignore_index=True)
157: 
158: 
159: def frame_for(big: pd.DataFrame, days: list[pd.Timestamp]) -> pd.DataFrame:
160:     """Rows of `big` whose origin is in `days`, sorted for a deterministic join order."""
161:     if not days:
162:         return big.iloc[:0]
163:     return (
164:         big[big["origin"].isin(pd.DatetimeIndex(sorted(days)))]
165:         .sort_values(["origin", "region", "hour"])

`usable_origins`: `origins(alerts["started_at"].min(), last)` then filter `o + 24h <= last`. First origin = Kyiv midnight of 2022-02-25 = 2022-02-24 22:00 UTC. `o + 24h = 2022-02-25 22:00 UTC <= last (2026-10-11 04:46)` → True → included. So the first origin's horizon starts at 2022-02-25 00:00 Kyiv, but data begins at 2022-02-25 16:36 UTC = 18:36 Kyiv. So the horizon [22:00 UTC 02-24, 22:00 UTC 02-25] contains the first ~6 hours unobserved (before 16:36 UTC). Its labels undercount → mild label noise for exactly one training day. ```python
first = alerts["started_at"].min()
return [o for o in origins(first, last) if first <= o and o + HOUR <= last]
```
`origins(first, last)` already starts at the first day, so `first <= o` is false for the first origin. That fixes it. Read file /home/agent/Code/KSE-Agentic-AI-stage2/src/evaluate.py, lines 180-259
180: 
181: 
182: def seasonal_naive_daily(history: pd.DataFrame, frame: pd.DataFrame, target: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
183:     """Baseline B summed over the day: `target` at hour - 7d per region (0.0 when unseen).
184: 
185:     `history` is every observed hour up to the eval window (train + tune, and the eval
186:     window's own earlier days). A naive baseline has no parameters, and its lookup
187:     `hour - 7d` is always strictly before the row's origin, so reading it is leak-free.
188:     Mirrors `models.seasonal_naive`, generalised to `y_minutes`.
189:     """
190:     lookup = history.groupby(["region", "hour"])[target].mean()
191:     key = pd.MultiIndex.from_frame(frame[["region", "hour"]].assign(hour=frame["hour"] - WEEK))
192:     out = frame[["origin", "region"]].copy()
193:     out["pred"] = lookup.reindex(key).fillna(0.0).to_numpy(np.float64)
194:     return out.groupby(["origin", "region"], as_index=False)["pred"].sum()
195: 
196: 
197: def mae(pred, actual) -> float:
198:     return float(np.mean(np.abs(np.asarray(pred) - np.asarray(actual))))
199: 
200: 
201: def rmse(pred, actual) -> float:
202:     d = np.asarray(pred) - np.asarray(actual)
203:     return float(np.sqrt(np.mean(d * d)))
204: 
205: 
206: def count_metrics(panel: pd.DataFrame, pred_col: str, actual_col: str) -> dict:
207:     """Per-region-day MAE/RMSE plus national (sum over regions) MAE per origin."""
208:     nat = panel.groupby("origin")[[pred_col, actual_col]].sum()
209:     return {
210:         "mae": mae(panel[pred_col], panel[actual_col]),
211:         "rmse": rmse(panel[pred_col], panel[actual_col]),
212:         "national_mae": mae(nat[pred_col], nat[actual_col]),
213:     }
214: 
215: 
216: def match_events(pred_times, actual_times, tol_min: int) -> tuple[int, int, int]:
217:     """Point-wise event match within +/- `tol_min`: returns (tp, fp, fn).
218: 
219:     tp = actual events with a prediction within tolerance; fp = predictions with no actual
220:     nearby; fn = actual events missed. Predictions sit on the hour (:00), so a start at
221:     h:20 is 20 min from the h:00 prediction — inside 30, outside 15.
222:     """
223:     p = np.sort(pd.DatetimeIndex(pred_times).as_unit("ns").asi8)  # ns: pandas 3 asi8 is not always ns
224:     a = pd.DatetimeIndex(actual_times).as_unit("ns").asi8
225:     tol = int(pd.Timedelta(minutes=tol_min).value)
226:     if len(a) == 0:
227:         return 0, int(len(p)), 0
228:     if len(p) == 0:
229:         return 0, 0, int(len(a))
230: 
231:     def within(src: np.ndarray, tgt: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
232:         i = np.searchsorted(src, tgt)
233:         left = np.clip(i - 1, 0, len(src) - 1)
234:         right = np.clip(i, 0, len(src) - 1)
235:         return np.minimum(np.abs(src[left] - tgt), np.abs(src[right] - tgt)) <= tol
236: 
237:     tp = int(within(p, a).sum())
238:     return tp, int((~within(a, p)).sum()), int(len(a) - tp)
239: 
240: 
241: def prf(tp: int, fp: int, fn: int) -> tuple[float, float, float]:
242:     p = tp / (tp + fp) if tp + fp else 0.0
243:     r = tp / (tp + fn) if tp + fn else 0.0
244:     f = 2 * p * r / (p + r) if p + r else 0.0
245:     return p, r, f
246: 
247: 
248: def actual_event_times(alerts: pd.DataFrame, start, end) -> dict:
249:     """Actual alert starts per region in `[start, end)`, at minute precision."""
250:     a = alerts[(alerts["started_at"] >= start) & (alerts["started_at"] < end)]
251:     return {region: pd.DatetimeIndex(g["started_at"]) for region, g in a.groupby("region")}
252: 
253: 
254: def _prob_groups(frame: pd.DataFrame, prob) -> list[tuple]:
255:     prob = np.asarray(prob)
256:     f = frame.reset_index(drop=True)
257:     return [(g["hour"].to_numpy(), prob[g.index.to_numpy()], region) for region, g in f.groupby("region")]
258:

1: # Findings — air-raid alert forecasting (Ukraine)
2: 
3: Time series analysis and forecasting of air-raid alerts in Ukraine, evaluated on the latest
4: data with a strict no-leakage setup. All numbers below come from `reports/metrics.json` or
5: `reports/eda.md` (both regenerated by the pipeline), except where a value is explicitly
6: marked as measured by an ad-hoc check.
7: 
8: ## 1. Question & setup
9: 
10: Three targets are forecast from one coherent setup:
11: 
12: 1. **Counts** — alert starts per region per day (`y_starts_day`), plus alert-minutes per
13:    region per day (`y_minutes_day`) as a secondary target.
14: 2. **Start timing** — a binary "an alert starts in this region × hour" for each of the next
15:    24 hours (`y_start`).
16: 3. **Duration** — log-duration of the next alert, modelled *conditional on a start*
17:    (`y_dur_log`), i.e. only on hours where a start actually happened.
18: 
19: - **Unit:** region × hour. **Origin:** 00:00 Europe/Kyiv, once per day. **Horizon:** the next
20:   24 h (origin + 0…23 h, UTC).
21: - **Data window and source:** 2022-02-25 → 2026-10-11 (UTC), 115,986 alert-starts, 24
22:   regions (`reports/eda.md`), from the volunteer snapshot `volunteer_data_en.csv` in
23:   `Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset` (see §2), plus Open-Meteo weather/daylight.
24:

54: 
55: Cleaning, and the numbers that drive it:
56: 
57: - **24 regions.** `Luhanska oblast` is dropped — 3 stray rows with no usable series
58:   (`src/features.py`); Luhansk and Crimea have permanent sirens and are absent upstream.
59:   The raw CSV has 115,989 rows; after the drop the evaluated table has 115,986 (`metrics.json`
60:   `data.rows`), consistent with 115,989 − 3.
61: - **5.0% right-censored starts.** `naive=5.01%` (`eda.md`); their end never arrived and the
62:   end is imputed upstream as start + 30 min. `GOAL.md` notes exactly 50% of alerts are ≤30 min,
63:   so these are right-censored durations: the **start event is kept**, the **duration is
64:   excluded** from duration labels. Duration-unusable (naive or >24 h) is `5.02%` (`eda.md`).
65: - No overlapping consecutive alerts per region (`GOAL.md`), so no de-duplication pass is needed.

74:   (volume over time), showing the regime shift.
75: - ![gaps and quiet](figures/gaps_and_quiet.png) `gaps_and_quiet.png` — distribution of gaps
76:   between consecutive alerts and of long quiet stretches.
77: - ![region burden](figures/region_burden.png) `region_burden.png` — per-oblast alert burden
78:   ranking (front-line oblasts dominate).
79: - ![durations](figures/durations.png) `durations.png` — alert-duration distribution, with
80:   the ≤30 min pile-up that makes `naive` rows censored.

85:   year 49.1 / 52.2 / 69.2 / 67.5 / 111.8 for 2022–2026. Change point 2026-08-26 (28-day
86:   window): mean 117.5 → 151.0 starts/day.

88: - **Gaps:** median **3.61 h**, p90 **17.16 h** (119 days max); 12% of gaps <1 h and 6% >24 h.
89:   Longest current quiet stretch: 73.0 days.
90: - **Top-5 regions by burden:** Donetska (605,958 min, 8,546 starts, 506.8/100d), Zaporizka
91:   (462,884 min, 8,969 starts, 531.3/100d), Dnipropetrovska (451,966 min, 13,728 starts,

93:   8,737 starts, 517.7/100d).

95:   Ivano-Frankivska (50,156 min, 893 starts, 53.0/100d), Zakarpatska (46,136 min, 761 starts,
96:   47.5/100d).
97: 
98: ## 3. Evaluation protocol
99: 
100: Splits are exactly as recorded in `reports/metrics.json` (`setup` and `splits`). 1,688 daily
101: origins have a fully observed 24 h horizon.
102: 
103: | Split | Kyiv dates (origin local) | Origins | `metrics.json` range (UTC) |
104: |---|---|---|---|
105: | `train` | 2022-02-26 → 2026-09-12 | 1,660 | `2022-02-25T22:00:00+00:00` → `2026-09-11T21:00:00+00:00` |
106: | `tune` | 2026-09-13 → 2026-09-26 | 14 | `2026-09-12T21:00:00+00:00` → `2026-09-25T21:00:00+00:00` |
107: | `test` (holdout) | 2026-09-27 → 2026-10-10 | 14 | `2026-09-26T21:00:00+00:00` → `2026-10-09T21:00:00+00:00` |
108: 
109: - **180-day rolling training window.** Every model and baseline fits on the last 180 origins
110:   strictly before the tune window (`holdout_train_window`: `2026-03-16T22:00:00+00:00` →
111:   `2026-09-11T21:00:00+00:00`), not on all history. This is deliberate: the series is
112:   non-stationary (§6), so a shorter recent window beats a long one.
113: - **3 rolling-origin folds**, 14 days each, stepping back 14 days (`metrics.json` `folds`):
114:   eval windows (UTC) fold 0 2026-08-29 → 2026-09-11, fold 1 2026-08-15 → 2026-08-28, fold 2
115:   2026-08-01 → 2026-08-14, each training on the 180 origins before it. **Folds are used ONLY to
116:   choose hyperparameters** (Poisson `alpha`, logistic `C`, HistGB learning-rate/iterations); no
117:   fold overlaps `tune` or `test`.
118: - **Frozen before the holdout.** Thresholds (`hour_of_week` 0.1, `seasonal_naive` 0.05,
119:   `logistic` 0.25, `histgb` 0.3) and split-conformal widths (`duration_naive` 1.304,
120:   `histgb_q50` 1.199) are fit on `train` → `tune` only.
121: - **`test` is read exactly once.** In `src/evaluate.py` the single
122:   `build_frames(splits["test"])` call is the first and only read; everything above it uses
123:   `train`, `tune` or fold windows.
124: 
125: Metrics and why each was picked:
126: 
127: - **Counts:** MAE and RMSE per region-day, and national MAE (sum over regions per origin).
128:   MAE in starts/day is directly comparable to the seasonal-naive baseline; RMSE shows whether
129:   mass-attack days dominate the error.
130: - **Start events:** precision / recall / F1 within **±15** and **±30 min**. A start is an
131:   event, so a point-wise tolerance match rewards predicting the right hour without spamming
132:   every hour; ±30 min covers the fact that predictions sit on the hour while starts happen at
133:   arbitrary minutes.
134: - **Duration:** MAE on **log**-duration (durations are skewed) plus **empirical split-conformal
135:   coverage** against the nominal 80%, so the intervals are checked for honesty, not just the
136:   point error.
137: - **Calibration:** a reliability curve of predicted vs observed start frequency, and predicted
138:   vs observed alert-time share per oblast.
139: 
140: ## 4. Results vs baselines
141: 
142: Holdout (`test`), from `reports/metrics.json`. "—" = target not applicable to that model.
143: 
144: | Model | starts MAE /day | starts nat. MAE | minutes MAE /day | F1 @±30 min | Brier | dur. log-MAE | coverage |
145: |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
146: | Baseline A — hour-of-week | 2.388 | 30.153 | — | 0.480 | 0.165 | — | — |
147: | Baseline B — seasonal naive | 2.244 | 22.286 | 106.435 | 0.404 | 0.292 | — | — |
148: | Model 1 — Poisson (counts) | 2.032 | 13.295 | 84.656 | — | — | — | — |
149: | Model 2 — HistGB (counts) | **1.527** | **11.384** | **79.562** | — | — | — | — |
150: | Model 1 — logistic (events) | — | — | — | **0.535** | 0.152 | — | — |
151: | Model 2 — HistGB (events) | — | — | — | 0.531 | **0.150** | — | — |
152: | Duration — train median | — | — | — | — | — | 0.849 | 77.4% |
153: | Duration — HistGB q50 | — | — | — | — | — | **0.791** | 76.9% |
154: 
155: `beats_baselines` in `metrics.json`: Poisson and HistGB beat both baselines on starts/day;
156: seasonal naive beats hour-of-week (2.244 < 2.388); hour-of-week beats neither.
157: 
158: - ![national daily](figures/eval_national_daily.png) `eval_national_daily.png` — national
159:   daily starts on the holdout: actual vs both baselines, Poisson and HistGB.
160: - ![calibration](figures/eval_calibration.png) `eval_calibration.png` — reliability curve for
161:   logistic and HistGB; both roughly track the diagonal and deviate most in the highest bins.
162: - ![region share](figures/eval_region_share.png) `eval_region_share.png` — per-oblast observed
163:   vs predicted alert-time share; points sit near the diagonal.
164: - ![duration conformal](figures/eval_duration_conformal.png) `eval_duration_conformal.png` —
165:   HistGB q50 duration predictions with 80% conformal intervals, sorted by prediction.
166: - ![fold scores](figures/eval_fold_scores.png) `eval_fold_scores.png` — rolling-origin fold
167:   MAE and F1, the evidence used for hyperparameter choice.
168: 
169: **Verdict.** Some things beat "alerts happen at the usual times", and some did not.
170: 
171: - **(a) Counts are won by the learners.** HistGB gives **MAE 1.527** starts per region-day
172:   versus seasonal naive **2.244** and hour-of-week **2.388**; national MAE 11.384 vs 22.286
173:   and 30.153. Poisson also beats both baselines (2.032). Minutes/day follows the same order
174:   (HistGB 79.562, Poisson 84.656, seasonal naive 106.435).
175: - **(b) Start events are a tie at the top.** HistGB (F1@±30 **0.531**) and the logistic
176:   Model 1 (**0.535**) are effectively tied and both clearly beat hour-of-week **0.480** and
177:   seasonal naive **0.404**. Within ±15 min nobody is good (F1 0.217–0.296): the hour is
178:   predictable, the minute is not.
179: - **(c) Duration barely improves.** Both duration models only slightly beat the train-median
180:   baseline: log-MAE **0.791** (HistGB) vs **0.849** (median), and **76.9%** empirical conformal
181:   coverage against a nominal **80%** — it **under-covers**, and that is reported as such.
182: - **(d) The hour-of-week baseline runs low.** Its national MAE is **30.153** starts/day and
183:   the errors are one-signed, so it under-predicts the holdout by the same ~30 starts/day
184:   nationally. That is the 2026 regime still rising (2026 volume is 2.28× 2022), i.e.
185:   non-stationarity, not a bug.
186: 
187: ## 5. Leakage checklist — "what is known at forecast time"
188: 
189: Every feature family is either sliced to `< origin` or is deterministic ahead of time.
190: 
191: | Feature family | Timestamp rule | Why it is safe |
192: |---|---|---|
193: | Calendar: `hour_local`, `day_of_week`, `is_weekend`, `month`, `is_holiday` | Derived from the forecast hour itself (calendar is deterministic, fixed holidays as month-day) | Known years ahead; reading the horizon's calendar is not a leak |
194: | Grid lags: `starts_past_1h/6h/24h/72h`, `alert_minutes_past_24h`, `trailing_28d_starts_per_day`, `hours_since_last_alert` | From the hourly grid / alert rows with `hour`/`started_at` **< origin**, asserted by `_guard` | Only past observations enter; no future alert can contribute |
195: | National: `regions_alerting_past_3h` | Counts grid rows with `hour` **< origin** and `minutes > 0`, asserted by `_guard` | Nationwide mass-attack signal uses only the last 3 h before origin |
196: | Weather lags: `temp_mean_24h`, `cloud_mean_24h`, `wind_mean_24h`, `precip_sum_24h` | Last 24 weather rows with `time` **< origin**, asserted by `_guard` | Past-24 h observed weather only |
197: | Daylight: `minutes_to_sunset`, `is_night`, `day_length_min` | Sunrise/sunset astronomy for each forecast hour; deliberately **not** guarded | Sunrise/sunset are deterministic and known years ahead |
198: 
199: **Enforcement.** Each family slices its source to `< origin` and asserts via the single
200: `_guard` helper that nothing at/after origin remains; that assert is the only path train and
201: evaluation share (`src/features.build`). Labels (`y_start`, `y_minutes`, `y_dur_log`) are the
202: only thing allowed to look forward and never pass through `_guard`. The linear models' NaN
203: imputation uses the **train median only** (`src/models.fit_predict`), and no scaler or encoder
204: is fit outside the training window — features are used raw.
205: 
206: **Independent check that was actually run.** Rebuilding a feature frame for holdout origins
207: with *every* source (alerts, grid, weather) truncated at `origin` leaves all **17
208: data-derived features** bit-identical to the un-truncated build. The astronomy daylight family
209: moves by a few minutes — up to **5 min** on the holdout origins (`minutes_to_sunset` ≤3 min,
210: `day_length_min` ≤5 min, and one night/day flag can flip) — because outside the weather file's
211: coverage the builder falls back to the previous day's astronomy. That is legitimately known
212: ahead, not leakage.
213: 
214: ## 6. Honest limitations
215: 
216: - **Volunteer-sourced data.** The upstream snapshot is community-maintained; missing or
217:   duplicated sirens are possible and are not modelled.
218: - **Right-censored ends.** 5.0% of starts are `naive` and are excluded from duration labels,
219:   so durations are learned on the uncensored majority only.
220: - **Weather as past-24 h lags.** Weather enters as lagged observations, not as a forecast read
221:   at origin. `ponytail: weather as past-24h lags; upgrade path: Open-Meteo forecast API read at
222:   origin.`
223: - **Daylight fallback.** Outside the weather coverage the builder reuses the previous day's
224:   astronomy (measured ≤5 min drift on the holdout origins).
225: - **Oblast granularity.** Everything is at oblast level, which hides the post-Dec-2025
226:   raion/hromada regime change; `official_data_en.csv` was rejected precisely because it breaks
227:   granularity inside the holdout window.
228: - **Luhansk and Crimea absent** (permanent sirens upstream), and the 3 stray Luhansk rows are
229:   dropped.
230: - **180-day rolling training window is a deliberate deviation** from GOAL.md's "everything up
231:   to T-28d", because the series is non-stationary; a long expanding window would mix regimes.
232: - **One logistic hyperparameter was uninformative.** `C` scored identically (F1 ≈ 0.522 / 0.537
233:   / 0.525 by fold) across the fold grid, so its choice carried no real signal.
234: - **Snapshot, not the API.** `alerts.in.ua` / `api.ukrainealarm.com` were unavailable (no
235:   token). `ponytail: snapshot source; upgrade path: alerts.in.ua client when a token exists.`
236: 
237: ## 7. Reproduce
238: 
239: From the repo root, with `uv`:
240: 
241: ```sh
242: uv run python -m src.fetch      # downloads alerts + weather, refreshes data/raw/manifest.json
243: uv run python -m src.eda        # writes reports/eda.md and the EDA figures
244: uv run python -m src.evaluate   # writes reports/metrics.json and the eval_* figures
245: uv run pytest -q                # self-checks
246: ```
247: 
248: `python -m src.evaluate` rewrites `reports/metrics.json` and the `eval_*` figures in
249: `reports/figures/` deterministically (fixed seeds, sorted joins, UTC timestamps), so re-running
250: it reproduces the numbers in this report.

## What was built

| Phase | File | Verified by me |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | `pyproject.toml` + `uv.lock` (uv 0.13, Python 3.14, pandas 3.0.6 / sklearn 1.9.1) | import smoke test |
| 1 | `src/fetch.py` — snapshot + Open-Meteo, idempotent, `manifest.json` | re-run leaves mtimes unchanged; manifest has upstream SHA, sha256, row/ts stats |
| 2 | `src/features.py` — origin-guarded builder (17 data features + 3 astronomy, 3 targets) | **truncate-everything-at-origin leak test: 0 differing cells**; manual recompute of lags/labels matches exactly |
| 3 | `src/eda.py` + `src/models.py` | 6 figures + `eda.md` numbers reproduce from raw; 24 regions, 5.01% censored, 2.28× volume ratio |
| 4 | `src/evaluate.py` — splits, 3 rolling folds, metrics, conformal, 5 figures | baselines recomputed from the raw CSV match `metrics.json` to 5–6 decimals; two full runs → identical stdout |
| 5 | `reports/findings.md` | every quoted number re-checked against `metrics.json` / `eda.md` / `manifest.json` |

## Two real bugs my verification caught (both fixed + regression-tested)

1. **`fit_predict` returned class labels, not probabilities.** `LogisticRegression.predict()` gives 0/1, so Model 1's "probabilities" were exactly 0/1 and its Brier (0.213) was *worse* than a constant predictor (0.172). After using `predict_proba`: F1@±30 **0.455 → 0.535** (now the best model on start events), Brier 0.213 → 0.152, and the calibration curve became a real curve.
2. **The first training origin had an incomplete 24 h horizon** (it starts before the record does), silently undercounting one day's labels. One-line guard added.

## Results on the holdout (Kyiv 2026-09-27 → 2026-10-10)

- **Counts/day:** HistGB **1.53** MAE < Poisson 2.03 < seasonal-naive 2.24 < hour-of-week 2.39 (per region-day).
- **Start events:** logistic 0.535 ≈ HistGB 0.531 ≫ hour-of-week 0.480 (F1@±30 min); ±15 min nobody is good — the hour is predictable, the minute is not.
- **Duration:** HistGB q50 0.791 vs median baseline 0.849 log-MAE; conformal coverage **76.9% vs nominal 80%** — under-covering, reported as such.
- The hour-of-week baseline under-predicts by ~30 starts/day nationally: the 2026 regime is still rising, not a bug.

Two notes: `python -m src.evaluate` takes ~3.5 min, and it's deterministic (two runs, byte-identical stdout). The manifest's `fetched_at` is wall-clock, so re-running `src.fetch` refreshes that one timestamp.

## What we built

A complete, reproducible pipeline that forecasts **air-raid alerts in Ukraine** from real data: 115,986 alert records (24 oblasts, Feb 2022 → Oct 11 2026) plus 4.5 years of hourly weather, evaluated honestly on the most recent 14 days with zero data leakage.

## What the scripts do (run in this order)

| Script | What it does |
|---|---|
| `python -m src.fetch` | Downloads the alert snapshot from GitHub + Open-Meteo weather into `data/raw/` (never committed). Idempotent — re-running skips unchanged files. Writes `manifest.json` with source URL, upstream git SHA, fetch time, row counts, timestamp ranges. |
| `python -m src.eda` | Exploration: 6 figures (daily volume, hour-of-week heatmap, monthly burden, gap distribution, per-oblast burden, durations) + `reports/eda.md` with the numbers. |
| `python -m src.features` | The core: builds one feature row per region × hour for a given forecast origin. Every feature is derived only from data *before* the origin, and an assert enforces it. Three targets: daily alert counts, "does an alert start in this hour", and next-alert duration. |
| `python -m src.models` | The model ladder: Baseline A (hour-of-week profile), Baseline B (seasonal naive, same hour last week), Model 1 (Poisson + logistic regression — interpretable), Model 2 (gradient boosting). |
| `python -m src.evaluate` | Splits the data (train / tune / 14-day holdout), runs a 3-fold rolling-origin backtest to pick hyperparameters, computes all metrics vs baselines, conformal duration intervals, calibration, and writes `reports/metrics.json` + 5 figures. ~3.5 min. |
| `reports/findings.md` | The write-up: provenance, protocol, results, an explicit "what is known at forecast time" leakage table, and honest limitations. |

## Headline findings (holdout = last 14 days)

- **Counts:** gradient boosting wins — MAE **1.53** alerts/region-day vs 2.24 for seasonal-naive and 2.39 for hour-of-week.
- **Alert timing:** logistic and boosting tie at F1 ≈ **0.53** within ±30 min (vs 0.48 baseline). Within ±15 min nobody is good — the hour is predictable, the minute isn't.
- **Duration:** barely beatable (log-MAE 0.79 vs 0.85 baseline); its 80% prediction intervals cover only 76.9% — honest under-coverage.
- **The story:** 2026 volume is 2.28× 2022, so "alerts happen at the usual times" systematically under-predicts the present by ~30 starts/day nationally. Models that use recent lags track the rising regime; the static baseline doesn't.

## Status

Definition of done met: 38 tests pass, `python -m src.fetch && python -m src.evaluate` reproduces the reported numbers, data is gitignored, and everything is committed in 6 phase commits.

Want me to plan a next step (e.g. a duration model per region, a real forecast-at-origin weather read, or publishing to GitHub)?

## 5. Reproduce / re-run

```sh

uv run pytest -q                # 38 self-checks
```

## One gap worth knowing

Per-row predictions are **not** persisted — `metrics.json` holds only aggregates, so you can't currently ask "which region-day was worst?" or "where does HistGB fail?" without writing a throwaway script that refits the models. If you want that kind of investigation, the natural next step is a small `reports/predictions.csv` dump from `src/evaluate.py` (origin, region, hour, actual, predicted for every model) plus maybe one error-breakdown figure.

1: # Findings — air-raid alert forecasting (Ukraine)
2: 
3: Time series analysis and forecasting of air-raid alerts in Ukraine, evaluated on the latest
4: data with a strict no-leakage setup. All numbers below come from `reports/metrics.json` or
5: `reports/eda.md` (both regenerated by the pipeline), except where a value is explicitly
6: marked as measured by an ad-hoc check.
7: 
8: ## 1. Question & setup
9: 
10: Three targets are forecast from one coherent setup:
11: 
12: 1. **Counts** — alert starts per region per day (`y_starts_day`), plus alert-minutes per
13:    region per day (`y_minutes_day`) as a secondary target.
14: 2. **Start timing** — a binary "an alert starts in this region × hour" for each of the next
15:    24 hours (`y_start`).
16: 3. **Duration** — log-duration of the next alert, modelled *conditional on a start*
17:    (`y_dur_log`), i.e. only on hours where a start actually happened.
18: 
19: - **Unit:** region × hour. **Origin:** 00:00 Europe/Kyiv, once per day. **Horizon:** the next
20:   24 h (origin + 0…23 h, UTC).
21: - **Data window and source:** 2022-02-25 → 2026-10-11 (UTC), 115,986 alert-starts, 24
22:   regions (`reports/eda.md`), from the volunteer snapshot `volunteer_data_en.csv` in
23:   `Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset` (see §2), plus Open-Meteo weather/daylight.
24: 
25: ## 2. Data & provenance
26: 
27: Everything used is recorded in `data/raw/manifest.json`; the values below are from the run
28: that produced this report (`fetched_at` `2026-10-11T12:11:00.935883+00:00` — re-running
29: `python -m src.fetch` refreshes that timestamp and the manifest).

55: 
56: Cleaning, and the numbers that drive it:
57: 
58: - **24 regions.** `Luhanska oblast` is dropped — 3 stray rows with no usable series
59:   (`src/features.py`); Luhansk and Crimea have permanent sirens and are absent upstream.
60:   The raw CSV has 115,989 rows; after the drop the evaluated table has 115,986 (`metrics.json`
61:   `data.rows`), consistent with 115,989 − 3.
62: - **5.0% right-censored starts.** `naive=5.01%` (`eda.md`); their end never arrived and the
63:   end is imputed upstream as start + 30 min. `GOAL.md` notes exactly 50% of alerts are ≤30 min,
64:   so these are right-censored durations: the **start event is kept**, the **duration is
65:   excluded** from duration labels. Duration-unusable (naive or >24 h) is `5.02%` (`eda.md`).
66: - No overlapping consecutive alerts per region (`GOAL.md`), so no de-duplication pass is needed.
67: 
68: **Exploration figures** (`reports/figures/`, produced by `python -m src.eda`):
69:

71:   day over the whole window; the rising 2026 level is visible.
72: - ![hour-of-week heatmap](figures/hour_of_week_heatmap.png) `hour_of_week_heatmap.png` —
73:   start intensity by local weekday × hour; peak at **Mon 12:00** Europe/Kyiv.
74: - ![monthly burden](figures/monthly_burden.png) `monthly_burden.png` — monthly alert burden
75:   (volume over time), showing the regime shift.
76: - ![gaps and quiet](figures/gaps_and_quiet.png) `gaps_and_quiet.png` — distribution of gaps
77:   between consecutive alerts and of long quiet stretches.
78: - ![region burden](figures/region_burden.png) `region_burden.png` — per-oblast alert burden
79:   ranking (front-line oblasts dominate).
80: - ![durations](figures/durations.png) `durations.png` — alert-duration distribution, with
81:   the ≤30 min pile-up that makes `naive` rows censored.

83: Key EDA numbers (`reports/eda.md`):

86:   year 49.1 / 52.2 / 69.2 / 67.5 / 111.8 for 2022–2026. Change point 2026-08-26 (28-day
87:   window): mean 117.5 → 151.0 starts/day.
88: - **Peak local hour-of-week:** **Mon 12:00**.
89: - **Gaps:** median **3.61 h**, p90 **17.16 h** (119 days max); 12% of gaps <1 h and 6% >24 h.
90:   Longest current quiet stretch: 73.0 days.
91: - **Top-5 regions by burden:** Donetska (605,958 min, 8,546 starts, 506.8/100d), Zaporizka
92:   (462,884 min, 8,969 starts, 531.3/100d), Dnipropetrovska (451,966 min, 13,728 starts,
93:   813.9/100d), Kharkivska (422,681 min, 12,980 starts, 769.0/100d), Sumska (358,914 min,
94:   8,737 starts, 517.7/100d).
95: - **Bottom-3 regions by burden:** Chernivetska (60,643 min, 914 starts, 54.2/100d),
96:   Ivano-Frankivska (50,156 min, 893 starts, 53.0/100d), Zakarpatska (46,136 min, 761 starts,
97:   47.5/100d).
98: 
99: ## 3. Evaluation protocol
100: 
101: Splits are exactly as recorded in `reports/metrics.json` (`setup` and `splits`). 1,688 daily
102: origins have a fully observed 24 h horizon.
103: 
104: | Split | Kyiv dates (origin local) | Origins | `metrics.json` range (UTC) |
105: |---|---|---|---|
106: | `train` | 2022-02-26 → 2026-09-12 | 1,660 | `2022-02-25T22:00:00+00:00` → `2026-09-11T21:00:00+00:00` |
107: | `tune` | 2026-09-13 → 2026-09-26 | 14 | `2026-09-12T21:00:00+00:00` → `2026-09-25T21:00:00+00:00` |
108: | `test` (holdout) | 2026-09-27 → 2026-10-10 | 14 | `2026-09-26T21:00:00+00:00` → `2026-10-09T21:00:00+00:00` |
109: 
110: - **180-day rolling training window.** Every model and baseline fits on the last 180 origins
111:   strictly before the tune window (`holdout_train_window`: `2026-03-16T22:00:00+00:00` →
112:   `2026-09-11T21:00:00+00:00`), not on all history. This is deliberate: the series is
113:   non-stationary (§6), so a shorter recent window beats a long one.
114: - **3 rolling-origin folds**, 14 days each, stepping back 14 days (`metrics.json` `folds`):
115:   eval windows (UTC) fold 0 2026-08-29 → 2026-09-11, fold 1 2026-08-15 → 2026-08-28, fold 2
116:   2026-08-01 → 2026-08-14, each training on the 180 origins before it. **Folds are used ONLY to
117:   choose hyperparameters** (Poisson `alpha`, logistic `C`, HistGB learning-rate/iterations); no
118:   fold overlaps `tune` or `test`.
119: - **Frozen before the holdout.** Thresholds (`hour_of_week` 0.1, `seasonal_naive` 0.05,
120:   `logistic` 0.25, `histgb` 0.3) and split-conformal widths (`duration_naive` 1.304,
121:   `histgb_q50` 1.199) are fit on `train` → `tune` only.
122: - **`test` is read exactly once.** In `src/evaluate.py` the single
123:   `build_frames(splits["test"])` call is the first and only read; everything above it uses
124:   `train`, `tune` or fold windows.
125: 
126: Metrics and why each was picked:
127: 
128: - **Counts:** MAE and RMSE per region-day, and national MAE (sum over regions per origin).
129:   MAE in starts/day is directly comparable to the seasonal-naive baseline; RMSE shows whether
130:   mass-attack days dominate the error.
131: - **Start events:** precision / recall / F1 within **±15** and **±30 min**. A start is an
132:   event, so a point-wise tolerance match rewards predicting the right hour without spamming
133:   every hour; ±30 min covers the fact that predictions sit on the hour while starts happen at
134:   arbitrary minutes.
135: - **Duration:** MAE on **log**-duration (durations are skewed) plus **empirical split-conformal
136:   coverage** against the nominal 80%, so the intervals are checked for honesty, not just the
137:   point error.
138: - **Calibration:** a reliability curve of predicted vs observed start frequency, and predicted
139:   vs observed alert-time share per oblast.
140: 
141: ## 4. Results vs baselines
142: 
143: Holdout (`test`), from `reports/metrics.json`. "—" = target not applicable to that model.
144: 
145: | Model | starts MAE /day | starts nat. MAE | minutes MAE /day | F1 @±30 min | Brier | dur. log-MAE | coverage |
146: |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
147: | Baseline A — hour-of-week | 2.388 | 30.153 | — | 0.480 | 0.165 | — | — |
148: | Baseline B — seasonal naive | 2.244 | 22.286 | 106.435 | 0.404 | 0.292 | — | — |
149: | Model 1 — Poisson (counts) | 2.032 | 13.295 | 84.656 | — | — | — | — |
150: | Model 2 — HistGB (counts) | **1.527** | **11.384** | **79.562** | — | — | — | — |
151: | Model 1 — logistic (events) | — | — | — | **0.535** | 0.152 | — | — |
152: | Model 2 — HistGB (events) | — | — | — | 0.531 | **0.150** | — | — |
153: | Duration — train median | — | — | — | — | — | 0.849 | 77.4% |
154: | Duration — HistGB q50 | — | — | — | — | — | **0.791** | 76.9% |
155: 
156: `beats_baselines` in `metrics.json`: Poisson and HistGB beat both baselines on starts/day;
157: seasonal naive beats hour-of-week (2.244 < 2.388); hour-of-week beats neither.
158: 
159: - ![national daily](figures/eval_national_daily.png) `eval_national_daily.png` — national
160:   daily starts on the holdout: actual vs both baselines, Poisson and HistGB.
161: - ![calibration](figures/eval_calibration.png) `eval_calibration.png` — reliability curve for
162:   logistic and HistGB; both roughly track the diagonal and deviate most in the highest bins.
163: - ![region share](figures/eval_region_share.png) `eval_region_share.png` — per-oblast observed
164:   vs predicted alert-time share; points sit near the diagonal.
165: - ![duration conformal](figures/eval_duration_conformal.png) `eval_duration_conformal.png` —
166:   HistGB q50 duration predictions with 80% conformal intervals, sorted by prediction.
167: - ![fold scores](figures/eval_fold_scores.png) `eval_fold_scores.png` — rolling-origin fold
168:   MAE and F1, the evidence used for hyperparameter choice.
169: 
170: **Verdict.** Some things beat "alerts happen at the usual times", and some did not.
171: 
172: - **(a) Counts are won by the learners.** HistGB gives **MAE 1.527** starts per region-day
173:   versus seasonal naive **2.244** and hour-of-week **2.388**; national MAE 11.384 vs 22.286
174:   and 30.153. Poisson also beats both baselines (2.032). Minutes/day follows the same order
175:   (HistGB 79.562, Poisson 84.656, seasonal naive 106.435).
176: - **(b) Start events are a tie at the top.** HistGB (F1@±30 **0.531**) and the logistic
177:   Model 1 (**0.535**) are effectively tied and both clearly beat hour-of-week **0.480** and
178:   seasonal naive **0.404**. Within ±15 min nobody is good (F1 0.217–0.296): the hour is
179:   predictable, the minute is not.
180: - **(c) Duration barely improves.** Both duration models only slightly beat the train-median
181:   baseline: log-MAE **0.791** (HistGB) vs **0.849** (median), and **76.9%** empirical conformal
182:   coverage against a nominal **80%** — it **under-covers**, and that is reported as such.
183: - **(d) The hour-of-week baseline runs low.** Its national MAE is **30.153** starts/day and
184:   the errors are one-signed, so it under-predicts the holdout by the same ~30 starts/day
185:   nationally. That is the 2026 regime still rising (2026 volume is 2.28× 2022), i.e.
186:   non-stationarity, not a bug.
187: 
188: ## 5. Leakage checklist — "what is known at forecast time"
189: 
190: Every feature family is either sliced to `< origin` or is deterministic ahead of time.
191: 
192: | Feature family | Timestamp rule | Why it is safe |
193: |---|---|---|
194: | Calendar: `hour_local`, `day_of_week`, `is_weekend`, `month`, `is_holiday` | Derived from the forecast hour itself (calendar is deterministic, fixed holidays as month-day) | Known years ahead; reading the horizon's calendar is not a leak |
195: | Grid lags: `starts_past_1h/6h/24h/72h`, `alert_minutes_past_24h`, `trailing_28d_starts_per_day`, `hours_since_last_alert` | From the hourly grid / alert rows with `hour`/`started_at` **< origin**, asserted by `_guard` | Only past observations enter; no future alert can contribute |
196: | National: `regions_alerting_past_3h` | Counts grid rows with `hour` **< origin** and `minutes > 0`, asserted by `_guard` | Nationwide mass-attack signal uses only the last 3 h before origin |
197: | Weather lags: `temp_mean_24h`, `cloud_mean_24h`, `wind_mean_24h`, `precip_sum_24h` | Last 24 weather rows with `time` **< origin**, asserted by `_guard` | Past-24 h observed weather only |
198: | Daylight: `minutes_to_sunset`, `is_night`, `day_length_min` | Sunrise/sunset astronomy for each forecast hour; deliberately **not** guarded | Sunrise/sunset are deterministic and known years ahead |
199: 
200: **Enforcement.** Each family slices its source to `< origin` and asserts via the single
201: `_guard` helper that nothing at/after origin remains; that assert is the only path train and
202: evaluation share (`src/features.build`). Labels (`y_start`, `y_minutes`, `y_dur_log`) are the
203: only thing allowed to look forward and never pass through `_guard`. The linear models' NaN
204: imputation uses the **train median only** (`src/models.fit_predict`), and no scaler or encoder
205: is fit outside the training window — features are used raw.
206: 
207: **Independent check that was actually run.** Rebuilding a feature frame for holdout origins
208: with *every* source (alerts, grid, weather) truncated at `origin` leaves all **17
209: data-derived features** bit-identical to the un-truncated build. The astronomy daylight family
210: moves by a few minutes — up to **5 min** on the holdout origins (`minutes_to_sunset` ≤3 min,
211: `day_length_min` ≤5 min, and one night/day flag can flip) — because outside the weather file's
212: coverage the builder falls back to the previous day's astronomy. That is legitimately known
213: ahead, not leakage.
214: 
215: ## 6. Honest limitations
216: 
217: - **Volunteer-sourced data.** The upstream snapshot is community-maintained; missing or
218:   duplicated sirens are possible and are not modelled.
219: - **Right-censored ends.** 5.0% of starts are `naive` and are excluded from duration labels,
220:   so durations are learned on the uncensored majority only.
221: - **Weather as past-24 h lags.** Weather enters as lagged observations, not as a forecast read
222:   at origin. `ponytail: weather as past-24h lags; upgrade path: Open-Meteo forecast API read at
223:   origin.`
224: - **Daylight fallback.** Outside the weather coverage the builder reuses the previous day's
225:   astronomy (measured ≤5 min drift on the holdout origins).
226: - **Oblast granularity.** Everything is at oblast level, which hides the post-Dec-2025
227:   raion/hromada regime change; `official_data_en.csv` was rejected precisely because it breaks
228:   granularity inside the holdout window.
229: - **Luhansk and Crimea absent** (permanent sirens upstream), and the 3 stray Luhansk rows are
230:   dropped.
231: - **180-day rolling training window is a deliberate deviation** from GOAL.md's "everything up
232:   to T-28d", because the series is non-stationary; a long expanding window would mix regimes.
233: - **One logistic hyperparameter was uninformative.** `C` scored identically (F1 ≈ 0.522 / 0.537
234:   / 0.525 by fold) across the fold grid, so its choice carried no real signal.
235: - **Snapshot, not the API.** `alerts.in.ua` / `api.ukrainealarm.com` were unavailable (no
236:   token). `ponytail: snapshot source; upgrade path: alerts.in.ua client when a token exists.`
237: 
238: ## 7. Reproduce
239: 
240: From the repo root, with `uv`:
241: 
242: ```sh
243: uv run python -m src.fetch      # downloads alerts + weather, refreshes data/raw/manifest.json
244: uv run python -m src.eda        # writes reports/eda.md and the EDA figures
245: uv run python -m src.evaluate   # writes reports/metrics.json and the eval_* figures
246: uv run pytest -q                # self-checks
247: ```
248: 
249: `python -m src.evaluate` rewrites `reports/metrics.json` and the `eval_*` figures in
250: `reports/figures/` deterministically (fixed seeds, sorted joins, UTC timestamps), so re-running
251: it reproduces the numbers in this report.

# EDA — air-raid alerts (national + per oblast)

All numbers below are produced by `python -m src.eda`; figures in `reports/figures/`.

## Data
- Window: 2022-02-25 → 2026-10-11 (UTC), 115,986 alert-starts, 24 regions.
- Censored: naive=5.01%; duration unusable (naive or >24h)=5.02%.

## Volume / regime
- Starts/day by year: 2022 49.1, 2023 52.2, 2024 69.2, 2025 67.5, 2026 111.8.
- 2026 vs 2022 volume ratio: **2.28×** (per-day, partial-year safe).
- Change point (2026-08-26, 28-day window): mean 117.5 → 151.0 starts/day (upward shift).

## Seasonality
- Peak local hour-of-week: **Mon 12:00** (Europe/Kyiv).
- Median gap between consecutive alerts: **3.61 h**; p90 gap: **17.16 h** (119 days max).

## Per-oblast burden (sorted by total known alert-minutes)
- Top 5:
  - Donetska oblast: 605,958 min, 8,546 starts, 506.8 starts/100d
  - Zaporizka oblast: 462,884 min, 8,969 starts, 531.3 starts/100d
  - Dnipropetrovska oblast: 451,966 min, 13,728 starts, 813.9 starts/100d
  - Kharkivska oblast: 422,681 min, 12,980 starts, 769.0 starts/100d
  - Sumska oblast: 358,914 min, 8,737 starts, 517.7 starts/100d
- Bottom 3:
  - Chernivetska oblast: 60,643 min, 914 starts, 54.2 starts/100d
  - Ivano-Frankivska oblast: 50,156 min, 893 starts, 53.0 starts/100d
  - Zakarpatska oblast: 46,136 min, 761 starts, 47.5 starts/100d

## Clustering
- 12% of gaps are <1 h; 6% are >24 h → repeated starts inside mass attacks plus long quiet tails.
- Longest current quiet stretch: 73.0 days.

## Figures
- `daily_starts.png`, `hour_of_week_heatmap.png`, `monthly_burden.png`, `gaps_and_quiet.png`, `region_burden.png`, `durations.png`

Sections (each 2-5 lines, total ~1 screen / ~40 lines):

1. **What this is** — "This is a record of every air-raid siren in Ukraine's 24 regions from 25 Feb 2022 to 11 Oct 2026 — 115,986 alerts in total. All times are Kyiv time."
2. **Life under sirens** — "A typical day brings about112 alert starts across the country (2026 average; 49 in 2022). In the worst 10% of days there are ≥ X. About Y% of days have at least one alert somewhere." Actually: use median/p90 national starts/day + share of days with alerts in front-line region.
3. **When alerts happen** — "Alerts are most likely around 12:00 on Monday, and least likely around 03:00 on Sunday. Z% of alerts start at night (22:00–06:00). Half of all alerts start within N hours of the previous one" (gaps: median 3.6h).
4. **How long alerts last** — "Half of alerts are over within 30 minutes... wait, 50% are ≤30 min (censored caveat). 9 out of 10 end within X minutes. A small share run for hours." Use quantiles from uncensored durations, plus a plain note that 5% of records have no recorded end.
5. **Where** — "Front-line regions live under sirens: Donetsk ~X hours/day on average since 2022, Zaporizhia..., while Zakarpattia/Chernivtsi ~Y hours/day." Compute alert-minutes/day per region. Top3 + bottom 3.
6. **Is it getting worse?** — "2026 has about 2.3× the daily alert volume of 2022 — the war shifted from missiles to mass drone waves, which means more, shorter alerts." Plain phrasing: "it catches most alerts but the hour it names is right only about half the time" — using F1? No, use precision & recall separately, they translate natively: "of the hours it flagged, X in 10 really had an alert; of all alerts, it flagged the right hour for Y in 10". And "which exact minute — no."
