## Goal

One command gives anyone a readable, jargon-free briefing: what the sirens data says, **and** what the models got right/wrong versus reality, with no statistical vocabulary (no MAE/F1/conformal/holdout/leakage).

## Files (3 touched, 2 new)

**1. `src/evaluate.py` — +3 lines.** The holdout prediction panel already exists in `main()` (`y_starts_day`, `p_histgb_start`, `p_snaive_start`, `p_how`, `p_poisson_start`, `p_*_min`, per `origin × region`); it currently dies after the figures. Dump it once to `reports/predictions.csv` (14 days × 24 regions = 336 rows). That's the "prediction vs reality" raw material — without it the brief could only show aggregate scores, and the user explicitly wants to see the model's misses next to what actually happened.

**2. `src/brief.py` (new, ~150 lines) — `python -m src.brief`.**
- Reads `data/raw/alerts_snapshot.csv` via the existing `features.load_alerts` (no second loader), `reports/predictions.csv`, and `reports/metrics.json`.
- Prints the brief to the terminal and writes the identical text to `reports/brief.md`.
- Missing `predictions.csv`/`metrics.json` → prints the descriptive part and one line: "run `python -m src.evaluate` to add the forecast-vs-reality part". No refitting, no network, no new dependencies.
- All wording is fixed templates with rounded numbers (counts integer, hours 1 decimal, probabilities as "X in 10"). Times shown in Kyiv time.

**3. `tests/test_brief.py` (new, small).** Synthetic alerts + tiny prediction frame → `build()` output is checked two ways: expected numbers/phrases present, and a banned-words assert (`MAE`, `RMSE`, `F1`, `Brier`, `conformal`, `holdout`, `leakage`, `stationar`, `baseline`, `poisson`, `histgb`) so jargon can't creep back in.

**4. `reports/findings.md` — 2 lines:** link to `reports/brief.md` near the top, and `python -m src.brief` in the Reproduce block. `findings.md` stays the technical report (AGENTS.md's definition of done is untouched). `reports/brief.md` gets committed so it's readable without running anything.

## The brief's shape (~1 screen + 2 tables)

```
# Air-raid alerts in Ukraine — plain-language brief
115,986 alerts, 24 regions, 25 Feb 2022 → 11 Oct 2026. Times are Kyiv time.

## Life under sirens
2026 brings about 112 alert starts a day across the country — 49 a day in 2022, so 2.3× more.
Half of all alerts are over within 30 minutes; 9 out of 10 within ~3 hours.

## When alerts happen
Most common: Monday around noon. Least common: Sunday before dawn.
About 3 in 10 alerts start at night (22:00–06:00). ...

## Where it is worst
| Region | Hours under sirens per day (average since 2022) |
| Donetsk | 4.2 |
| Zaporizhia | 3.8 |
| ... (top 3 + bottom 3) |

## How well can this be predicted?
Tested on the last 14 days of data — the models never saw these days while learning.
- How many alerts per day: the model is off by about 1.5 per region, the simple
  "guess a typical day" is off by 2.2. Countrywide: ~11 wrong vs ~22.
- Day-by-day table: Date | What really happened | Model's guess | Simple guess
  (each row is a one-day-ahead guess, checked against the day that followed)
- When an alert starts: of the hours the model flagged as risky, about 3 in 10 really
  had one; it flagged the right hour for about 8 in 10 real alerts. Which minute — no.
- How long it lasts: guesses are off by about 20 minutes on average, barely better
  than always answering "the usual length" (~21 min). Our "between X and Y minutes"
  range turned out right about 77 times in 100.

## What this is not
Not an early-warning system · oblast-wide (no city/street) · volunteer-collected,
some alerts missing · Luhansk & Crimea are absent from the data.
```

Every number there maps to something already computed (`metrics.json`, `predictions.csv`, or a few pandas ops on the alerts CSV: durations quantiles, night share, hour-of-week peak, alert-minutes/day per region, starts/day by year). The "off by" wording is literally `mae` in starts/day and minutes, translated — never named.

Good thing I checked — I had misquoted the timing numbers in the draft. Here's exactly what the brief will say the model accomplished, in the words it will use:

**The test: the last 14 days of data (27 Sep – 10 Oct 2026). The models were not allowed to see those days while learning, and each guess was made "the day before", then checked against what really happened.**

### 1. "How many alerts tomorrow?" — real win ✅
> Guessing "a typical recent day" is wrong by about **2.2 alerts per region per day**. The model is wrong by about **1.5**. Countrywide: **~11 alerts/day off vs ~22** for the simple guess — half the error.

That's the model's genuine accomplishment: the count of alerts is meaningfully more predictable than "it's usually like this".

### 2. "Which hour will an alert start?" — half useful ⚠️
> Of the hours the model flags as risky, **about half really have an alert** — and it flags the right hour for **about half of all real alerts**. The simple guesses manage only 4 in 10 both ways. It cannot name the minute.

So: better than the naive guesses, but when it rings its virtual bell, it's right about as often as not. It gives a risk picture ("today is looking heavy"), not a timetable.

### 3. "How long will the next alert last?" — no real accomplishment ❌
> Its length guesses are off by about **20 minutes on average — versus 21 minutes for always answering "the usual length"**. Its "between X and Y minutes" range turns out right **77 times in 100** (it aims for 80).

Honest verdict: predicting duration added almost nothing over common sense, and the brief says so in those words.

### The one-sentence summary the brief opens with
> The model learned **how many** alerts to expect and **roughly which hours** are risky — it did **not** learn to pin down the exact minute, and it learned **nothing useful** about how long an alert will last.

What's *not* claimed: no early-warning ability, no "air raid at 14:03 in Kharkiv". The brief's numbers are the ones above, translated (no MAE/F1/coverage words — "wrong by X alerts", "right N times in 100").

1: """Honest holdout evaluation for the air-raid alert forecasts.
2: 
3: Order of operations — every choice is frozen on train/tune/folds before `test` is read:
4: 
5:     usable_origins -> split_days -> rolling_folds
6:     build train + tune + fold frames        (no test origin is built)
7:     choose_hyperparameters(folds)           (model selection only)
8:     fit on the 180-day train window -> predict tune
9:     choose_threshold + conformal_width(tune)
10:     build test frames                       <-- FIRST and ONLY read of `test`
11:     fit on train -> predict test -> metrics -> figures -> reports/metrics.json
12: 
13: The single test read is the `build_frames(splits["test"])` call in `main`; everything above
14: it uses `train`, `tune` or the fold windows, so no threshold/width/hyperparameter can have
15: seen a test label.
16: 
17: ponytail: 180-day rolling train window for every model and baseline so all numbers are
18: comparable and every fit stays in seconds (the series is non-stationary, 2026 volume is
19: 2.28x 2022); upgrade path: expanding window with time-decay sample weights.

72: 
73: def usable_origins(alerts: pd.DataFrame) -> list[pd.Timestamp]:
74:     """Daily origins whose whole 24 h horizon is observed: `origin + 24h <= max(finished_at)`.
75: 
76:     The last alerts end ~2026-10-11 04:46 UTC, so the last usable origin is Kyiv 2026-10-10.
77:     """
78:     last = alerts["finished_at"].max()
79:     first = alerts["started_at"].min()
80:     # The first day's horizon begins before the record does, so its labels would undercount.
81:     return [o for o in origins(first, last) if first <= o and o + HOUR <= last]
82: 
83: 
84: def _between(all_origins: list[pd.Timestamp], start, end) -> list[pd.Timestamp]:
85:     return [o for o in all_origins if start <= o < end]
86: 
87: 
88: def train_window(all_origins: list[pd.Timestamp], end, days: int = TRAIN_DAYS) -> list[pd.Timestamp]:
89:     """The last `days` daily origins strictly before `end` (rolling window, same for all)."""
90:     return _between(all_origins, end - pd.Timedelta(days=days), end)
91: 
92: 
93: def split_days(alerts: pd.DataFrame, test_days: int = TEST_DAYS, tune_days: int = TUNE_DAYS) -> dict:
94:     """Time-ordered, disjoint, non-overlapping windows: train < tune < test.
95: 
96:     `test` is the last `test_days` usable origins, `tune` the `tune_days` before it, `train`
97:     everything earlier. Nothing is shuffled.
98:     """
99:     usable = usable_origins(alerts)
100:     test = usable[-test_days:]
101:     tune = usable[-test_days - tune_days : -test_days]
102:     train = usable[: -test_days - tune_days]
103:     return {
104:         "train": train,
105:         "tune": tune,
106:         "test": test,
107:         "train_end": train[-1],
108:         "tune_start": tune[0],
109:         "test_start": test[0],
110:     }
111: 
112: 
113: def rolling_folds(
114:     alerts: pd.DataFrame, n_folds: int = N_FOLDS, fold_days: int = FOLD_DAYS, train_days: int = TRAIN_DAYS
115: ) -> list[dict]:

94:     df = pd.read_csv(path, usecols=["region", "started_at", "finished_at", "naive"])

110: def load_weather(directory: str | pathlib.Path = DEFAULT_WEATHER) -> pd.DataFrame:
111:     """Cached, read-only hourly weather for every region (`time` UTC-aware)."""
112:     frames = []

115:         for col in ("time", "sunrise", "sunset"):
116:             df[col] = pd.to_datetime(df[col], utc=True)
117:         df["region"] = SLUG_TO_REGION.get(path.stem, path.stem)

123: def hourly_grid(alerts: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
124:     """Dense `region × hour` grid: alert-minutes overlapping each hour and starts counts.
125: 
126:     The single source behind every lag and label: minutes split alert-seconds across the
127:     hour buckets it spans, starts counts alerts whose `started_at` floors into the hour.
128:     """
129:     a = alerts[["region", "started_at", "finished_at"]].reset_index(drop=True)
130: 
131:     def naive(s: pd.Series) -> np.ndarray:
132:         return s.dt.tz_convert("UTC").dt.tz_localize(None).to_numpy()
133: 
134:     start, finish = naive(a["started_at"]), naive(a["finished_at"])
135:     start_h = a["started_at"].dt.floor("h")
136:     end_h = a["finished_at"].dt.floor("h")
137:     n = (((end_h - start_h).dt.total_seconds() // 3600).astype("int64") + 1).clip(lower=1)
138:     n_arr = n.to_numpy()
139:     idx = np.repeat(np.arange(len(a)), n_arr)
140:     offs = np.arange(len(idx)) - np.repeat(np.cumsum(n_arr) - n_arr, n_arr)
141:     hour = naive(start_h)[idx] + offs * np.timedelta64(1, "h")
142:     minutes = (np.minimum(finish[idx], hour + np.timedelta64(1, "h")) - np.maximum(start[idx], hour))

Read file /home/agent/Code/KSE-Agentic-AI-stage2/src/models.py, lines 1-167
1: """Model ladder for the alert forecasts: two baselines, then two learners.
2: 
3: Both learners read the same `FEATURES` matrix built by `src/features.build` (the daily
4: learners read its day-level subset, `DAY_FEATURES`), so the report can compare *learners*
5: rather than two feature pipelines. Everything here is a pure function or an sklearn
6: factory: no classes, no state outside the fitted sklearn estimator, nothing fitted on
7: anything but the training window.
8: """
9: 
10: from __future__ import annotations
11: 
12: import numpy as np
13: import pandas as pd
14: from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingRegressor
15: from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression, PoissonRegressor
16: 
17: from src.features import FEATURES
18: 
19: # Daily targets have one row per origin x region, so the hourly features carry no
20: # day-level information: `hour_local` is constant-noise across the collapse,
21: # `minutes_to_sunset`/`is_night` describe one specific hour of the 24. They must not enter
22: # a daily model (they would fit the arbitrary "first hour" of the day). `day_of_week`,
23: # `is_weekend`, `month`, `is_holiday`, `day_length_min` are properties of the origin's
24: # local calendar day, not of an hour, so they stay.
25: HOUR_FEATURES = ["hour_local", "minutes_to_sunset", "is_night"]
26: DAY_FEATURES = [f for f in FEATURES if f not in HOUR_FEATURES]
27: 
28: _PROFILE_KEYS = ["region", "day_of_week", "hour_local"]
29: 
30: 
31: def daily_panel(frame: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
32:     """Collapse a `build()` frame to one row per origin x region.
33: 
34:     Day features are taken verbatim from the origin's first (00:00 local) hour row, not
35:     aggregated, so the panel describes the calendar day the forecast is made on. Labels are
36:     summed over the 24 forecast hours. No row is fabricated: the collapsed index is exactly
37:     the origins x regions present in `frame`.
38:     """
39:     first = frame.sort_values(["origin", "region", "hour"]).groupby(["origin", "region"], as_index=False).head(1)
40:     sums = frame.groupby(["origin", "region"], as_index=False).agg(
41:         y_starts_day=("y_start", "sum"), y_minutes_day=("y_minutes", "sum")
42:     )
43:     out = first[["origin", "region", *DAY_FEATURES]].merge(sums, on=["origin", "region"], how="inner")
44:     return out.sort_values(["origin", "region"]).reset_index(drop=True)
45: 
46: 
47: def hour_of_week_profile(train_frame: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
48:     """Baseline A: empirical P(alert starts) per (region, local weekday, local hour).
49: 
50:     Fit on training rows only. The two fallback levels are precomputed here as broadcast
51:     columns, so prediction is one lookup and one map — no recompute that could see eval data.
52:     """
53:     cells = train_frame.groupby(_PROFILE_KEYS, as_index=False)["y_start"].mean().rename(columns={"y_start": "p"})
54:     cells["p_region"] = cells["region"].map(train_frame.groupby("region")["y_start"].mean())
55:     cells["p_global"] = float(train_frame["y_start"].mean())
56:     return cells
57: 
58: 
59: def predict_hour_of_week(profile: pd.DataFrame, frame: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:
60:     """Baseline A prediction: cell mean -> region mean -> global mean. Length == len(frame).
61: 
62:     The two fallback levels live as broadcast columns of `profile`, so they are read with a
63:     separate map instead of the cell merge (a left join would NaN them out on an unseen cell).
64:     """
65:     cells = profile[_PROFILE_KEYS + ["p"]]
66:     region_mean = profile.drop_duplicates("region").set_index("region")["p_region"]
67:     global_mean = float(profile["p_global"].iloc[0]) if len(profile) else 0.0
68:     merged = frame[_PROFILE_KEYS].merge(cells, on=_PROFILE_KEYS, how="left")
69:     filled = merged["p"].fillna(merged["region"].map(region_mean)).fillna(global_mean)
70:     return filled.to_numpy(np.float64)
71: 
72: 
73: def seasonal_naive(train_frame: pd.DataFrame, frame: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:
74:     """Baseline B: the `y_start` observed at hour - 7 days for the same region; 0.0 if the
75:     training window does not cover that timestamp (quiet regime / start of the series)."""
76:     lookup = train_frame.groupby(["region", "hour"])["y_start"].mean()
77:     key = pd.MultiIndex.from_frame(frame[["region", "hour"]].assign(hour=frame["hour"] - pd.Timedelta("7D")))
78:     return lookup.reindex(key).fillna(0.0).to_numpy(np.float64)
79: 
80: 
81: def duration_rows(frame: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
82:     """Rows where the duration label is defined: a start happened and was neither censored
83:     (`naive` end) nor implausibly long (>24 h), so excluding them avoids training on imputed
84:     ends."""
85:     return frame[(frame["y_start"] == 1) & frame["y_dur_log"].notna()]
86: 
87: 
88: def duration_naive(train_frame: pd.DataFrame, frame: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:
89:     """Duration baseline: training-window median log-duration-at-start, broadcast.
90: 
91:     ponytail: one constant per region-pooled series, no timing signal; upgrade path: make the
92:     median conditional on hour/region once the empirical quantiles show real spread.
93:     """
94:     median = duration_rows(train_frame)["y_dur_log"].median()
95:     return np.full(len(frame), 0.0 if pd.isna(median) else float(median))
96: 
97: 
98: def make_poisson(**kw) -> PoissonRegressor:
99:     """Model 1 (counts/minutes): log-link Poisson, coefficients expoundable as log-rate
100:     effects. Target must be >= 0 (y_starts_day, y_minutes_day). `alpha` is the L2 penalty
101:     that keeps the unscaled lag magnitudes in check. `newton-cholesky` because the raw
102:     (unscaled) lag features make lbfgs crawl (thousands of iterations); the exact Hessian
103:     converges in ~10 and is deterministic, so no seed is needed.
104:     ponytail: newton-cholesky factorizes a p x p matrix, so it wants features < rows;
105:     upgrade path: switch to lbfgs + a train-fitted scaler if the feature count ever grows.
106:     """
107:     params = dict(alpha=1.0, solver="newton-cholesky", max_iter=1000)
108:     params.update(kw)
109:     return PoissonRegressor(**params)
110: 
111: 
112: def make_logistic(**kw) -> LogisticRegression:
113:     """Model 1 (hourly start probability). Features are used raw: no scaler, so coefficients
114:     stay log-odds per original unit (a scaler fitted on train would be fine leakage-wise but
115:     would cost the interpretability that is the point of Model 1). `newton-cholesky` for the
116:     same reason as the Poisson fit. Seed fixed for the rare stochastic solvers.
117:     """
118:     params = dict(C=1.0, solver="newton-cholesky", max_iter=1000, random_state=0)
119:     params.update(kw)
120:     return LogisticRegression(**params)
121: 
122: 
123: def make_histgb(loss: str = "poisson", quantile: float | None = None, **kw) -> HistGradientBoostingRegressor:
124:     """Model 2: gradient boosting on the same matrix. `loss="poisson"` for counts/minutes,
125:     `loss="quantile"` with q in {0.1, 0.5, 0.9} for log-duration. Handles NaN natively, so
126:     the imputation in `fit_predict` is only needed by the linear models. `early_stopping` is
127:     off: an internal random validation split would make the fit depend on row order.
128:     """
129:     params = dict(
130:         loss=loss,
131:         learning_rate=0.05,
132:         max_iter=300,
133:         max_leaf_nodes=31,
134:         min_samples_leaf=20,
135:         l2_regularization=1.0,
136:         early_stopping=False,
137:         random_state=0,
138:     )
139:     if loss == "quantile":
140:         if quantile not in (0.1, 0.5, 0.9):
141:             raise ValueError(f"quantile must be 0.1/0.5/0.9, got {quantile!r}")
142:         params["quantile"] = quantile  # target range: log1p(minutes), ~[0, log(1440)]
143:     params.update(kw)
144:     return HistGradientBoostingRegressor(**params)
145: 
146: 
147: def fit_predict(model, train_frame: pd.DataFrame, frame: pd.DataFrame, target: str) -> np.ndarray:
148:     """Fit `model` on train rows, predict `frame` on the same feature matrix.
149: 
150:     Hourly frames carry all of `FEATURES`; daily panels carry `DAY_FEATURES`. Selecting the
151:     columns present keeps one code path for both. NaN is possible in `hours_since_last_alert`
152:     for the first days of the series; the linear models reject it, so it is filled with the
153:     train median (an imputer fit on the training window only — never on the eval frame).
154:     """
155:     cols = [c for c in FEATURES if c in train_frame.columns]
156:     assert len(cols) in (len(FEATURES), len(DAY_FEATURES)), "unexpected feature columns"
157:     X = train_frame[cols].to_numpy(np.float64)
158:     median = np.nanmedian(X, axis=0)
159:     median = np.where(np.isnan(median), 0.0, median)  # all-NaN column in train: 0 is as good as any
160:     model.fit(np.where(np.isnan(X), median, X), train_frame[target].to_numpy())
161:     X_eval = np.where(np.isnan(frame[cols].to_numpy(np.float64)), median, frame[cols].to_numpy(np.float64))
162:     # A classifier's `predict` returns class labels; every caller here wants the rate, so
163:     # take the positive-class probability instead (a hard 0/1 "probability" is useless for
164:     # thresholds, Brier and calibration).
165:     if hasattr(model, "predict_proba"):
166:         return model.predict_proba(X_eval)[:, 1]
167:     return model.predict(X_eval)

---

Let me write a helper:
```python
first, last = local.min().normalize(), local.max().normalize()
def year_rate(y):
    lo = max(first, pd.Timestamp(f"{y}-01-01", tz=KYIV))
    hi = min(last, pd.Timestamp(f"{y}-12-31", tz=KYIV))
    days = (hi - lo).days + 1
    return (alerts.loc[local.dt.year == y].shape[0] / days, days)
```

```python
DOW = ["Monday","Tuesday","Wednesday","Thursday","Friday","Saturday","Sunday"]
NIGHT = "22:00–06:00"


def _year_rate(alerts, local, year):
    lo = max(local.min().normalize(), pd.Timestamp(f"{year}-01-01", tz=KYIV))
    hi = min(local.max().normalize(), pd.Timestamp(f"{year}-12-31", tz=KYIV))
    days = (hi - lo).days + 1
    n = int((local.dt.year == year).sum())
    return n / days


def _peak_week_hour(local):
    cells = pd.DataFrame({"dow": local.dt.dayofweek, "hour": local.dt.hour})
    counts = cells.value_counts()
    wd_days = local.dt.normalize().drop_duplicates().dt.dayofweek.value_counts()
    rate = counts / wd_days.reindex(counts.index.get_level_values("dow")).to_numpy()
    (pd_, h_), _ = rate.idxmax()
    (qd, qh), _ = rate.idxmin()
    return f"{DOW[pd_]} around {pd_:02d}:00"  # careful variable naming
```
Better: peak = rate.idxmax() → tuple (dow, hour). Format f"{DOW[dow]} around {hour:02d}:00".

```python
def _region_hours(alerts, days):
    mins = alerts.groupby("region")["duration_min"].sum()
    return (mins / days / 60).sort_values(ascending=False)
```

```
# Air-raid alerts in Ukraine — the short version

115,986 air-raid alerts across 24 regions, 25 Feb 2022 → 11 Oct 2026. All times Kyiv time.
(Numbers marked "the test" come from the last 14 days of data — 27 Sep → 10 Oct 2026 —
which the forecast was not allowed to look at while learning.)

## Life under sirens
- 2026 brings about 112 siren starts a day across the country; in 2022 it was 49 — 2.3× more.
- Half of all alerts are over within 34 minutes; 9 in 10 within 4 hours.
- Sirens come in waves: in a given region, the next alert usually starts within 3½ hours of the previous one.
- About 3 in 10 alerts start at night (22:00–06:00).

## When
- Busiest time of the week: Monday around 12:00. Quietest: Sunday around 04:00.
- Of the 24 regions, Donetsk, Zaporizhzhia and Dnipropetrovsk spend the most time under sirens: ~5.9, 4.6 and 4.5 hours a day on average since 2022.
- The quietest: Zakarpattia ~0.5 h, Ivano-Frankivsk ~0.5 h, Chernivtsi ~0.6 h a day.

## What we tried to predict, and how it went
Test: 14 days, one forecast per region per day, each made the evening before.

**How many alerts to expect — the model genuinely helps.**
- Off by about 1.5 alerts per region per day; the simple "assume a typical recent day" guess is off by 2.2.
- Countrywide: about 11 alerts a day wrong, versus about 22 for the simple guess.

| Really happened | Model said | Simple guess said |
...
```

**Which hour an alert starts — half right.**

**How long an alert lasts — essentially not solved.**
> Length guesses are off by about 20 minutes; always answering "the usual length" is off by 21. The "between X and Y" range we gave held up about 77 times in 100.

## What this is not
- Not an early-warning system: it says "this day and this region look busy", not "sirens at 14:03".
- Oblast-wide only: no city, no street.
- Volunteer-collected: some alerts are missing; Luhansk and Crimea are not in the data (their sirens are effectively permanent).

```json
  "content": "\"\"\"A plain-language briefing on Ukraine's air-raid alerts and how well forecasts did.\n\nReads only files the pipeline already wrote — no network, no fitting, no wall-clock:\n\n    return f\"{hours:.1f}\"\n\n\ndef _duration(minutes: float) -> str:\n    return f\"{round(minutes)} minutes\" if minutes < 120 else f\"{minutes / 60:.1f} hours\"\n\n\ndef _year_rate(alerts: pd.DataFrame, local: pd.Series, year: int) -> float:\n    \"\"\"Alert starts per day inside `year`, over the days of that year the data covers.\"\"\"\n    lo = max(local.min().normalize(), pd.Timestamp(f\"{year}-01-01\", tz=KYIV))\n    hi = min(local.max().normalize(), pd.Timestamp(f\"{year}-12-31\", tz=KYIV))\n    days = (hi - lo).days + 1\n    return int((local.dt.year == year).sum()) often that weekday occurs in the window, otherwise a partial\n    final week would make some slots look artificially quiet.\n    \"\"\"\n    counts = pd.DataFrame({\"dow\": local.dt.dayofweek, \"hour\": local.dt.hour}).value_counts()\n    wd_days = local.dt.normalize().drop_duplicates().dt.dayofweek.value_counts()\n    rate = counts / wd_days.reindex(counts.index.get_level_values(\"dow\")).to_numpy()\n\n    def label(key) -> str:\n        dow, hour = key\n        return f\"{DOW[dow]} around {hour:02d}:00\"\n\n    return label(rate.idxmax()), label(rate.idxmin())\n\n\ndef _counts_lines(metrics: dict) -> list[str]:\n    counts = metrics[\"counts_daily\"][\"y_starts_day\"]\n    model, simple = counts[\"histgb\"], counts[\"seasonal_naive\"]\n    return [\n        \"**How many alerts to expect — the forecast genuinely helps.**\",\n        f\"- Off by about **{model['mae']:.1f} alerts** per region per day; the   f\"about {simple['national_mae']:.0f} for the simple guess.\",\n    ]\n\n\ndef _counts_table(preds: pd.DataFrame) -> list[str]:\n    day = pd.to_datetime(preds[\"origin\"], ].sum()\n    rows = [\"\", \"| Day | Sirens that really started | Model guessed | Simple guess |\", \"|---|---|---|---|\"]\n    rows += [\n        f\"| {d} | {r['y_starts_day']:.0f} | {r['p_histgb_start']:.0f} | {r['p_snaive_start']:.0f} |\"\n        for d, r in national.iterrows()\n    ]\n    return rows\n\n\ndef _region_table(preds: pd.DataFrame, top: int = 8) -> list[str]:\n    per_region = preds.groupby(\"region\")[[\"y_minutes_day\", \"p_histgb_min\"]].mean() / 60\n    per_region = per_region.sort_values(\"y_minutes_day\", ascending=False).head(top)\n    rows = [\"\", \"| Region | Hours under sirens per day (real) | Model guessed |\", \"|---|---|---|\"]\n    rows += [\n        f\"| {region} | {r['y_minutes_day']:.1f} | {r['p_histgb_min']:.1f} |\"\n        for region, r in per_region.iterrows()\n    ]\n    return rows\n\n\ndef _window_line(metrics: dict) -> str:\n    start, end = metrics[\"splits\"][\"test\"][0], metrics[\"splits\"][\"test\"][1]\n    def day(ts):\n        return pd.Timestamp(ts).tz_convert(KYIV).strftime(\"%d %b %Y\")\n    return f\"{day(start)} → {day(end)}\"\n\n\ndef _forecast_section(metrics: dict, preds: pd.DataFrame) -> list[str]:\n    events = metrics[\"start_events\"][\"histgb\"][\"tol_30\"]\n    simple = metrics[\"start_events\"][\"seasonal_naive\"][\"tol_30\"]\n    duration = metrics[\"duration\"][\"histgb_q50\"]\n    duration_simple = metrics[\"duration\"][\"duration_naive\"]\n\n    return [\n        f\"## What we tried to predict, and how it went\",\n        \"\",\n        f\"Test: the last 14 days of data ({_window_line(metrics)}). One forecast per region per \"\n        \"day, each made the evening before, then checked against what really happened. The \"\n        \"forecast never saw these days while learning.\",\n        \"\",\n        *_counts_lines(metrics),\n        *_counts_table(preds),\n        \"\",\n        \"**Which hour an alert starts — about half right.**\",\n        f\"- Of the hours flagged as risky, about **{events['precision'] * 10:.0f} in 10** really \"\n        f\"had an alert; it named the right hour for about {events['recall'] * 10:.0f} in 10 of the \"\n        f\"alerts that really happened. The simple guesses manage about \"\n        f\"{simple['precision'] * 10:.0f} in 10 either way.\",\n        \"- The exact minute is not predictable — nobody got close on that.\",\n        \"\",\n        \"**How long an alert lasts — essentially unsolved.**\",\n        f\"- Length guesses are off by about **{duration['mae_minutes']:.0f} minutes** on average; \"\n        f\"always answering \\\"the usual length\\\" is off by {duration_simple['mae_minutes']:.0f} \"\n        \"minutes. The \\\"between X and Y minutes\\\" range held up about \"\n        f\"{duration['coverage'] * 100:.0f} times in 100.\",\n        \"\",\n        f\"**Where the burden falls in the test period** (top {8} regions by siren time):\",\n        *_region_table(preds),\n    ]\n\n\ndef build(alerts: pd.DataFrame, metrics: dict | None, preds: pd.DataFrame | None) -> str:\n    local = alerts[\"started_at\"].dt.tz_convert(KYIV)\n    first, last = local.min().strftime(\"%d %b %Y\"), local.max().strftime(\"%d %b %Y\")\n    days = (local.max().normalize() - local.min().normalize()).days + 1\n\n    durations = alerts[\"duration_min\"].dropna()\n    median_d, p90_d = durations.quantile([0.5, 0.9])\n    night = float((local.dt.hour.isin([22, 23, 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5])).mean())\n    gaps = alerts.groupby(\"region\")[\"started_at\"].diff().dt.total_seconds().div(3600).dropna()\n    peak, quiet = _busiest_week_hour(local)\n    hours = (alerts.groupby(\"region\")[\"duration_min\"].sum() / days / 60).sort_values(ascending=False)\n\n    rate_2026, rate_2022 = _year_rate(alerts, local, 2026), _year_rate(alerts, local, 2022)\n    busiest = \", \".join(f\"{r} ~{_spell(h)} h\" for r, h in hours.head(3).items())\n    quietest = \", \".join(f\"{r} ~{_spell(h)} h\" for r, h in hours.tail(3).items())\n\n    lines = [\n        \"# Air-raid alerts in Ukraine — the short version\",\n        \"\",\n        f\"{len(alerts):,} air-raid alerts across {alerts['region'].nunique()} regions of Ukraine, \"\n        f\"{first} → {last}. All times are Kyiv time.\",\n        \"\",\n        \"## Life under sirens\",\n        f\"- 2026 brings about {rate_2026:.0f} siren starts a day across the country; in 2022 it \"\n        f\"was {rate_2022:.0f} — **{rate_2026 / rate_2022:.1f}× more**. Most of the rise is recent: \"\n        \"the daily average climbed again in late August 2026.\",\n        f\"- Half of all alerts are over within {_duration(median_d)}; 9 in 10 within \"\n        f\"{_duration(p90_d)}.\",\n        f\"- Alerts come in waves: in a region, the next siren usually starts within \"\n        f\"{_spell(gaps.median())} h of the previous one.\",\n        f\"- About {night * 10:.0f} in 10 alerts start at night (22:00–06:00).\",\n        \"\",\n        \"## When, and where, they happen\",\n        f\"- Busiest slot of the week: **{peak}**. Quietest: {quiet}.\",\n        f\"- Most siren time per day on average since 2022: {busiest}.\",\n        f\"- Least: {quietest}.\",\n        \"\",\n    ]\n\n    if metrics is not None and preds is not None:\n        lines += _forecast_section(metrics, preds)\n    else:\n        lines += [\n            \"## What we tried to predict, and how it went\",\n            \"\",\n            \"The forecast-vs-reality part needs `reports/predictions.csv` and \"\n            \"`reports/metrics.json`: run `python -m src.evaluate` first.\",\n            \"\",\n        ]\n\n    lines += [\n        \"## What this is not\",\n        \"- Not an early-warning system. It says a day and a region look busy; it cannot say \"\n        \"\\\"sirens at 14:03 in your street\\\".\",\n        \"- Oblast-wide only — no city, no district.\",\n        \"- Volunteer-collected data: some alerts are missing, and Luhansk and Crimea are absent \"\n        \"(their sirens are effectively permanent).\",\n        \"\",\n        \"Technical version, with the full scoring rules and the leakage checklist: \"\n        \"`reports/findings.md`. }
```

# Air-raid alerts in Ukraine — the short version

115,986 air-raid alerts across 24 regions of Ukraine, 25 Feb 2022 → 11 Oct 2026. All times are Kyiv time.

## Life under sirens
- 2026 brings about 112 siren starts a day across the country; in 2022 it was 49 — **2.3× more**. Most of the rise is recent: the daily average climbed again in late August 2026.
- Half of all alerts are over within 31 minutes; 9 in 10 within 107 minutes.
- Alerts come in waves: in a region, the next siren usually starts within 3.6 h of the previous one.
- About 3 in 10 alerts start at night (22:00–06:00).

## When, and where, they happen
- Busiest slot of the week: **Monday around 12:00**. Quietest: Sunday around 07:00.
- Most siren time per day on average since 2022: Donetska oblast ~6.0 h, Zaporizka oblast ~4.6 h, Dnipropetrovska oblast ~4.5 h.
- Least: Chernivetska oblast ~0.6 h, Ivano-Frankivska oblast ~0.5 h, Zakarpatska oblast ~0.5 h.

## What we tried to predict, and how it went

The forecast-vs-reality part needs `reports/predictions.csv` and `reports/metrics.json`: run `python -m src.evaluate` first.

## What this is not
- Not an early-warning system. It says a day and a region look busy; it cannot say "sirens at 14:03 in your street".
- Oblast-wide only — no city, no district.
- Volunteer-collected data: some alerts are missing, and Luhansk and Crimea are absent (their sirens are effectively permanent).

Technical version, with the full scoring rules and the leakage checklist: `reports/findings.md`. Figures: `reports/figures/`.

# Air-raid alerts in Ukraine — the short version

115,986 air-raid alerts across 24 regions of Ukraine, 25 Feb 2022 → 11 Oct 2026. All times are Kyiv time.

## Life under sirens
- 2026 brings about 112 siren starts a day across the country; in 2022 it was 49 — **2.3× more**. Most of the rise is recent: the daily average climbed again in late August 2026.
- Half of all alerts are over within 31 minutes; 9 in 10 within 107 minutes.
- Alerts come in waves: in a region, the next siren usually starts within 3.6 h of the previous one.
- About 3 in 10 alerts start at night (22:00–06:00).

## When, and where, they happen
- Busiest slot of the week: **Monday around 12:00**. Quietest: Sunday around 07:00.
- Most siren time per day on average since 2022: Donetska oblast ~6.0 h, Zaporizka oblast ~4.6 h, Dnipropetrovska oblast ~4.5 h.
- Least: Chernivetska oblast ~0.6 h, Ivano-Frankivska oblast ~0.5 h, Zakarpatska oblast ~0.5 h.

## What we tried to predict, and how it went

Test: the last 14 days of data (27 Sep 2026 → 10 Oct 2026). One forecast per region per day, each made the evening before, then checked against what really happened. The forecast never saw these days while learning.

**How many alerts to expect — the forecast genuinely helps.**
- Off by about **1.5 alerts** per region per day; the simple "assume a typical recent day" guess is off by 2.2.
- Countrywide: about **11 alerts a day** wrong, against about 22 for the simple guess.

| Day | Sirens that really started | Model guessed | Simple guess |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sun 27 Sep | 112 | 131 | 165 |
| Mon 28 Sep | 118 | 134 | 149 |
| Tue 29 Sep | 143 | 130 | 147 |
| Wed 30 Sep | 133 | 134 | 167 |
| Thu 01 Oct | 124 | 135 | 153 |
| Fri 02 Oct | 132 | 129 | 158 |
| Sat 03 Oct | 117 | 128 | 124 |
| Sun 04 Oct | 120 | 125 | 112 |
| Mon 05 Oct | 126 | 128 | 118 |
| Tue 06 Oct | 134 | 129 | 143 |
| Wed 07 Oct | 141 | 131 | 133 |
| Thu 08 Oct | 132 | 142 | 124 |
| Fri 09 Oct | 166 | 144 | 132 |
| Sat 10 Oct | 170 | 140 | 117 |

**Which hour an alert starts — about half right.**
- Of the hours flagged as risky, about **5 in 10** really had an alert; it named the right hour for about 5 in 10 of the alerts that really happened. The simple guesses manage about 4 in 10 either way.
- The exact minute is not predictable — nobody got close on that.

**How long an alert lasts — essentially unsolved.**
- Length guesses are off by about **20 minutes** on average; always answering "the usual length" is off by 21 minutes. The "between X and Y minutes" range held up about 77 times in 100.

**Where the burden falls in the test period** (top 8 regions by siren time):

| Region | Hours under sirens per day (real) | Model guessed |
|---|---|---|
| Kharkivska oblast | 6.3 | 5.4 |
| Mykolaivska oblast | 4.9 | 4.9 |
| Dnipropetrovska oblast | 4.7 | 5.1 |
| Kirovohradska oblast | 4.5 | 4.6 |
| Zaporizka oblast | 4.1 | 4.1 |
| Chernihivska oblast | 4.0 | 3.9 |
| Poltavska oblast | 3.8 | 4.4 |
| Kyivska oblast | 3.7 | 4.4 |
## What this is not
- Not an early-warning system. It says a day and a region look busy; it cannot say "sirens at 14:03 in your street".
- Oblast-wide only — no city, no district.
- Volunteer-collected data: some alerts are missing, and Luhansk and Crimea are absent (their sirens are effectively permanent).

Technical version, with the full scoring rules and the leakage checklist: `reports/findings.md`. Figures: `reports/figures/`.

# Air-raid alerts in Ukraine — the short version

115,986 air-raid alerts across 24 regions of Ukraine, 25 Feb 2022 → 11 Oct 2026. All times are Kyiv time.

## Life under sirens
- 2026 brings about 112 siren starts a day across the country; in 2022 it was 49 — **2.3× more**. Most of the rise is recent: the daily average climbed again in late August 2026.
- Half of all alerts are over within 31 minutes; 9 in 10 within 1.8 hours.
- Alerts come in waves: in a region, the next siren usually starts within 3.6 h of the previous one.
- About 3 in 10 alerts start at night (22:00–06:00).

## When, and where, they happen
- Busiest slot of the week: **Monday around 12:00**. Quietest: Sunday around 07:00.
- Most siren time per day on average since 2022: Donetska oblast ~6.0 h, Zaporizka oblast ~4.6 h, Dnipropetrovska oblast ~4.5 h.
- Least: Chernivetska oblast ~0.6 h, Ivano-Frankivska oblast ~0.5 h, Zakarpatska oblast ~0.5 h.

## What we tried to predict, and how it went

Test: the last 14 days of data (27 Sep 2026 → 10 Oct 2026). One forecast per region per day, each made the evening before, then checked against what really happened. The forecast never saw these days while learning.

**How many alerts to expect — the forecast genuinely helps.**
- Off by about **1.5 alerts** per region per day; the simple "assume a typical recent day" guess is off by 2.2.
- Countrywide: about **11 alerts a day** wrong, against about 22 for the simple guess.

| Day | Sirens that really started | Model guessed | Simple guess |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sun 27 Sep | 112 | 131 | 165 |
| Mon 28 Sep | 118 | 134 | 149 |

**Part 1: one command to run everything.**

Options:
- `src/run_all.py` calling each module's `main()` in-process, in order: fetch → eda → evaluate → brief.
- Or a shell script / Makefile.
- Or `python -m src.pipeline`.

Lazy choice: a tiny module `src/run_all.py` (~15 lines) that imports and calls the mains in order. Reuses mains. In-process is nicer than subprocess (no re-import cost, no shell). But: evaluate.py uses ProcessPoolExecutor with fork — works fine when imported (workers fork from the current process; it already warms caches in its main()). Calling `evaluate.main()` after `fetch.main()` in the same process: `load_alerts` is `lru_cache`d — if fetch refreshed the CSV after `load_alerts` was already called... In run_all order fetch runs first, and fetch's main presumably doesn't call load_alerts. But eda/evaluate do. Within one process, caches get populated after the fetch — fine. However, `features.load_alerts`/`load_grid`/`load_weather` are `lru_cache`d; if `eda.main()` loads and then `evaluate.main()` calls `load_grid()` "to warm caches", fine — same data.

Risk: any module holding module-level state or matplotlib backend issues — evaluate sets Agg at import; eda probably too. In-process is fine.

Alternatively subprocess: `subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-m", "src.fetch"], check=True)` for each — perfectly isolates, guaranteed same behavior as manual runs. Slightly slower (interpreter startup + pandas import ×4 ≈ +5-10s total). For a long pipeline (fetch network + evaluate minutes), negligible. And it's honestly simpler to reason about + exact reproduction of the documented commands. Hmm, lazy = shortest diff: in-process calls are ~10 lines; subprocess loop is ~8 lines. Both trivial. Subprocess guarantees identical semantics to running each command separately (no caching weirdness, no fork-after-import subtleties with mp). I'd pick subprocess with `check=True` and fail-fast. Actually in-process fails fast too.

Concern with in-process: `evaluate.main()` uses `mp`/ProcessPoolExecutor with default start method; on Linux fork — fine.

One subtle in-process hazard: `features._load_alerts_cached` is lru_cached on path; if `src.fetch` writes a new CSV and `eda` calls load_alerts after, fine. Order is fetch first anyway.

I'll propose in-process `src/run_all.py` calling the four `main()`s in order? Let me think again about which is better for reproducibility claim in findings.md: "python -m src.run_all reproduces everything". Subprocess per step means each step's behavior is byte-identical to the documented standalone commands. In-process means matplotlib figures/Font caches shared — deterministic anyway.

I lean subprocess: simplest to explain ("it runs exactly the four documented commands, in order"), no risk of module state interaction, trivially logs each command. ~10 lines. And fail-fast with check=True, propagate exit code.

Also ordering caveat: `src.brief` needs `reports/predictions.csv` from evaluate, so brief runs last. fetch is networked and idempotent. Should run_all include fetch? GOAL says `python -m src.fetch && python -m src.evaluate` produces the numbers. But re-fetching changes data → brief/findings numbers change. The user wants "runs all the needed scripts in order" — that's fetch → eda → evaluate → brief. Maybe add `--skip-fetch` flag? Ponytail: no flags... but reproducibility vs. re-fetch is a real distinction: fetch mutates inputs. Hmm. AGENTS.md definition of done: `python -m src.fetch && python -m src.evaluate`. So full run includes fetch. Keep run_all = fetch → eda → evaluate → brief, no flags (YAGNI). Note in docstring: "fetch refreshes the data; run the modules individually to skip it."

**1. `src/run_all.py` (new, ~15 lines).**
```python
"""Run the whole pipeline in order: fetch -> eda -> evaluate -> brief..."""
import subprocess, sys

STEPS = ("src.fetch", "src.eda", "src.evaluate", "src.brief")

def main() -> None:
    for step in STEPS:
        print(f"\n=== {step} ===", flush=True)
        subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-m", step], check=True)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
Subprocess per step = exactly the documented commands, fail-fast via check=True, exit code propagates (non-zero → traceback? check=True raises CalledProcessError → nonzero exit; fine). Use `sys.executable` so it's the same interpreter (uv venv). Command: `uv run python -m src.run_all`.

Note: don't preface with `uv` — the runner itself runs under uv.

Plan is small. Here it is:

## 1. `src/run_all.py` (new, ~15 lines)

Runs the four documented commands in order, each as a subprocess, fail-fast:

```
STEPS = ("src.fetch", "src.eda", "src.evaluate", "src.brief")
# for each: print a "=== src.fetch ===" banner, then
subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-m", step], check=True)
```

- Subprocess per step = byte-for-byte the same behavior as running the commands by hand (no shared module caches, no fork-after-import surprises), and a step failure stops the chain with that step's output intact.
- `sys.executable` keeps it inside whatever interpreter invoked it (`uv run python -m src.run_all`).
- Order matters and is the dependency order: `brief` reads `reports/predictions.csv` that `evaluate` writes.
- No `--no-fetch` flag (you picked the full chain); the docstring notes that `fetch` refreshes upstream data, so re-running can shift numbers — run the modules individually to skip it.

## 2. `src/brief.py` — 3 embedded figures (~10 lines)

One helper, no more:

```python
def _fig(name: str, caption: str) -> list[str]:
    return [f"![{caption}](figures/{name})", ""] if (FIGDIR / name).exists() else []
```

Missing file → the line is simply skipped, so `python -m src.brief` still works standalone before `eda`/`evaluate` have run. Placement, one per section:

| Section | Figure | Caption (plain words) |
|---|---|---|
| Life under sirens | `daily_starts.png` | Alert starts per day since Feb 2022 — the level keeps rising |
| When, and where | `hour_of_week_heatmap.png` | Alert starts by weekday and hour (Kyiv time): Monday noon is the busiest slot |
| What we tried to predict | `eval_national_daily.png` | Last 14 days: what really happened vs the model's guess vs the simple guess |

Relative `figures/…` links render as images in `reports/brief.md` on GitHub; in the terminal the same line just names the file, which keeps stdout == `brief.md` byte-identical. Captions go through the jargon test like everything else.

## 3. `tests/test_brief.py` — one more check

`_fig` returns `[]` when the figure is absent and the `![caption](figures/name)` line when a dummy file exists (monkeypatch `brief.FIGDIR` to `tmp_path`). Existing render + jargon tests keep passing since captions avoid the banned words.

## 4. Docs, two lines

- `reports/findings.md` Reproduce block: `uv run python -m src.run_all` first as the one-command run, per-step commands kept underneath.
- `AGENTS.md` Commands: one line `Full pipeline: python -m src.run_all`.

Skipped: terminal image rendering (iTerm/kitty escapes), HTML export, generating figures inside `brief.py` (they belong to `eda`/`evaluate`), a `--no-fetch` flag.

## What this is not

## Assistant

_Thinking:_

The user wants a plain explanation (in chat, no code): what the model found, did well, didn't do well, and an explanation of "our best model" vs "the simple model/guess" they keep seeing referenced. This is a conversational answer — no files to touch. Plan mode just means I can't modify files; answering questions is fine.

Let me answer in plain language, using the current numbers:

Best model = Model 2, gradient boosting (HistGradientBoostingRegressor) on lag/calendar/weather features — "the model".
Simple guess = baselines: Baseline A hour-of-week profile, Baseline B seasonal naive ("same hour last week"). In the brief I call them "the simple guess" (usually seasonal naive: assume a typical recent day / same as last week).
Also Model 1 = interpretable Poisson/logistic.

What it found/did well:
- Counts: best model wrong by ~1.5 alerts per region-day vs 2.2 for the simple guess; countrywide ~11 vs 22. So counting is learnable.
- Hour of alert start: about half right (5 in 10 flagged hours really had an alert, catches ~5-6 in 10 alerts), beats simple guesses (4 in 10).
- Patterns found: daily/weekly seasonality (Monday noon busiest), volume up 2.3x since 2022, front-line oblasts 4-6h/day under sirens, alerts come in waves (median gap 3.6h), half end within 30 min, night share 3 in 10.

What it didn't do well:
- Duration: ~19-20 min error vs 20-21 for "the usual length" — basically no gain; range right 77 in 100 vs 80 promised.
- Exact minute not predictable (F1 within ±15 min poor).
- hour-of-week baseline under-predicts due to rising volume (non-stationarity).
- Can't say where at city level; not an early-warning system.

Explain the two comparison baselines in plain terms:
1. "Simple guess" (seasonal naive): assume tomorrow at this hour looks like the same hour last week / a typical recent day.
2. Hour-of-week profile: average alert rate for that region+weekday+hour from training history ("alerts happen at the usual times").
And Model 1 (Poisson/logistic regression) = interpretable model, coefficients readable. Model 2 = gradient boosting = best.

Explain why we compare: to show the model learned something beyond "usual times" and not just memorized.

Keep it short and clear. No tools needed.

Here's the short version.

## What the model actually does
At midnight it looks at everything that happened before that moment and makes three guesses for the next 24 hours, per region: **how many alerts**, **which hours they'll start in**, and **how long the next one lasts**.

## Did well
- **Counting alerts.** This is the real win. On the last 14 days (days it never saw while learning) it was wrong by about **1.5 alerts per region-day**, versus **2.2** for the simple guess — countrywide ~11 wrong vs ~22. Roughly half the error.
- **Rough timing.** The hours it flags as risky really contain an alert about **5 times in 10**, and it names the right hour for about half of all real alerts — the simple guesses only manage 4 in 10.
- **Finding the patterns** (that's the analysis half, all solid): alerts cluster in waves (next one usually within ~3½ h), busiest slot is Monday noon, half of all alerts are over within 30 minutes, volume grew ~2.3× since 2022, and front-line oblasts live under sirens 4–6 hours a day while western ones see ~30 minutes.

## Didn't do well
- **Duration is basically unsolved.** Its length guesses are off by ~19–20 minutes, and just always answering "the usual length" is off by 20–21. That's no meaningful gain — so we report it as a failure, not a feature.
- **The exact minute is not predictable** — the hour yes, the minute no.
- **It can't see geography below oblast level**, and it says nothing about *where* a strike lands. It's a risk picture, not an early-warning system.

## The two comparison guesses you keep seeing
They exist to prove the model learned something and didn't just memorize the calendar:

1. **"The simple guess" (seasonal naive)** — assume today looks like the same hour **last week**, or in daily terms "a typical recent day". Zero learning, just copying recent history.
2. **Hour-of-week profile** — "alerts happen at the usual times": the average alert rate for that region, that weekday, that hour, from the training history.

If the fancy model can't beat those, it's useless — that's the whole point of the comparison. It does beat them on **how many** and **which hour**; it does *not* beat "just answer the usual length" on duration.

One more model sits in between: a deliberately simple, human-readable one (Poisson/logistic regression) so the numbers can be explained; the "best model" is the more powerful gradient-boosting learner, and both read the same inputs so the comparison is about the learner, not the data.

---

## User

is the second model better than human readable one?

---

## Assistant

_Thinking:_

The user asks: is the second model (HistGB, "real model") better than the human-readable one (Model 1: Poisson + logistic)?

From the latest metrics:
- Counts (starts/day): Poisson MAE 2.048, national 12.303; HistGB 1.533, national 10.931. So HistGB better. Minutes/day: Poisson 83.12 vs HistGB 78.59 → HistGB better.
- Events: logistic F1 0.538 vs HistGB 0.543 — basically tied (logistic was slightly ahead in the previous run). Brier: logistic 0.155 vs histgb 0.153 — tied.
- Duration: only HistGB (quantile) vs train-median baseline; no linear duration model. So not comparable.

So answer: yes on counts clearly (~25% less error), tied on hourly start probability, duration only the boosting model tried and it barely beats the dumb guess.

Also explain the tradeoff: interpretability vs accuracy; what the human-readable one gives (coefficients: which features push risk up/down) and that its counts are worse but still beat baselines. Nuance: for "how many" the boosted model is meaningfully better; for "which hour" the difference is within noise, so you could use the readable one there and lose nothing.

Keep short, plain.

Yes for counting, a tie for timing — and the readable one has no duration story at all.

| Question | Human-readable (Poisson / logistic) | Best model (gradient boosting) | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| How many alerts/day | wrong by **2.05** per region-day (countrywide 12.3) | wrong by **1.53** (countrywide 10.9) | Boosting clearly wins — ~25% less error |
| Which hour starts | 0.538 | 0.543 | Tie, within noise (the readable one even won the previous run) |
| How long | not built | barely beats "the usual length" | Nobody wins |

So: if you care about the **count** — the main deliverable — the second model is genuinely better and worth its black-box-ness. If you care about **which hours are risky**, the human-readable model is just as good, and you get the explanation for free: its coefficients say which ingredients push risk up (e.g. many regions alerting at once recently, high trailing 24-hour alert time) versus down.

Worth keeping in mind: both read exactly the same inputs, so the gap isn't "more data" — it's purely the learner being better at squeezing non-linear patterns out of them. And the honest caveat from the write-up: the readable model's answers are readable in *log-rate* terms, so "explainable" means a statistician can read them, not that a person on the street can.

Practical takeaway I'd stand behind: use boosting for the daily count, keep the readable model as the "why" companion — which is exactly how `findings.md` presents them.

## Draft content

```markdown
# How the models evolved

Short version of the development story behind `findings.md` §4: the readable model came first,
and the second model is an iteration on the same inputs, built to be more accurate.

## 1. The bar to clear: "alerts happen at the usual times"
Before any model, two naive guesses set the bar: the average alert rate for that region,
weekday and hour ("hour-of-week profile"), and "same hour last week" (seasonal naive). Any
model that cannot beat these is useless, no matter how fancy.

## 2. First model: human-readable (Poisson + logistic)
Same feature matrix as everything after it, but deliberately simple learners: a Poisson
regression for daily counts and a logistic regression for "an alert starts in this hour".
Goal: numbers a human can explain — coefficients show what pushes risk up (many regions
alerting at once in the last 3 hours, high trailing alert-time) and down.

Result: already beats both naive guesses on counts (wrong by 2.05 alerts/region-day vs 2.23
and 2.49; countrywide 12.3 vs 21.7 and 33.7) and is competitive on timing (0.54 vs 0.49 and
0.40). The lesson from this step: *how many* and *roughly which hour* are learnable at all.

## 3. Second model: gradient boosting, iterated
Same inputs, more expressive learner (gradient boosting on decision trees). It was not
one-shot: on 3 rolling-origin folds we swept learning rate x tree count (0.05/0.1 x 100/300)
and took the setting that won consistently across folds — 0.05 with 100 trees for counts,
300 for duration. Different loss per task: Poisson-style loss for counts and alert-minutes,
quantile loss for durations.

Result vs the first model:
| Question | Model 1 (readable) | Model 2 (boosting) | What the iteration bought |
|---|---|---|---|
| How many alerts/day | 2.05 wrong | **1.53 wrong** | ~25% less error; countrywide 12.3 -> 10.9 |
| Alert-minutes/day | 83 wrong | **79 wrong** | modest, same direction |
| Which hour starts | 0.54 | 0.54 | nothing — a tie |
| How long it lasts | not built | 0.80 vs 0.86 for the median guess | barely anything |

## 4. What we took away
- Use **Model 2 for the count** — the accuracy gain is real and it is the main deliverable.
- Use **Model 1 when the "why" matters** — it is just as good at hourly timing and its
  coefficients are readable; Model 2 cannot offer that.
- **Duration stays unsolved** for both approaches: neither beat answering "the usual length",
  and the promised ranges held up about 77 times in 100 instead of 80. Reported as a failure,
  not a feature.

Discipline that held across both iterations: one shared feature matrix (so the comparison is
about the learner, not the data), splits frozen in time order, folds used only for choosing
settings, and the last 14 days read exactly once.
```

Numbers are the current ones from `reports/metrics.json` (the 12 Oct snapshot), so it stays consistent with `findings.md` — and like everything else it refreshes with the artifacts only when someone re-runs and updates the text.

Switch agents and I'll write `reports/models.md`, add the two pointers, commit, and push.
